Measured on the author's install: **758 such entries, ~232 KB, 524 of them producing a card** (the remaining 234 are `system-prompt-digest`, which carries only a hash and no `content`, so it yields no notice). `noticeFromCustomMessage` filters exactly two things: `session_exit` (runtime plumbing with no prose) and the chamber's own `chamber-goal-state` / `chamber-plan-state` mode bookkeeping.
- **`display` is deliberately NOT the gate, in either direction.** Filtering on it would hide the goal injections (`GoalNotice` is the only slot for an objective and its budget), the loop guards (`thinking-loop-redirect` / `tool-call-loop-redirect` are the only record that a turn was cut short) and `ultrathink-notice` / `xdev-mount-notice` / `resolve-reminder` — all of them `display:false` in omp too. Do not "fix" the noise by adding a denylist: that decision was made and reversed, and a reader who wants the full transcript wins over a tidier one. If the volume ever needs addressing, the lever is a per-row collapse in `SystemNotice` (it already collapses by default), not a drop in the parser.
- **`eval-state-context` arrives in TWO entry shapes, and both must keep rendering.** omp 18.12.0 persists it at startup via `appendCustomMessageEntry` (it was in-memory only before), so a resumed session that ever used `eval` carries it — as `type:"custom_message"` in some builds and as `type:"message"` with `message.role:"custom"` in others. The second shape reaches the timeline through the assistant branch of `toChatMessage` (`messages-map.ts`), not through `noticeFromCustomMessage`, which is why both paths are pinned separately.
- **A `narration` thinking block is answer text, and the split lives in the shared parser.** omp 18.12.0 marks prose the model addressed to the user as `narration: true` on a `thinking` block (decoded from the Anthropic/Bedrock thinking signature), and every omp surface presents it as reply text (`presentNarrationAsAnswer`, used by the TUI's streaming reveal, print mode and the ACP mapper). The Kabmo folded every `thinking` block into the collapsed "Thinking" accordion, so a reply sent between two tool calls vanished from the transcript. `parseMessageBlocks` (`parse-message-blocks.ts`) now pushes a narrated body into `textParts` and leaves `thinking` untouched — stated once, because both the live fold and the JSONL reload read it. Only the literal `true` promotes a block; a truthy string or a provider's own marker must not move reasoning into the reply. `narration.test.ts` covers the core rule, the block ordering, the `text` fallback and both adapters end-to-end.

### Process Supervision (`proc://`, and the legacy `hub` tool)

- **`proc://` is omp's device for background jobs, project services and live agents, and it is reached through the `read`/`write` TRANSPORT — so the URL, never the tool name, decides the panel.** omp replaced the `hub` tool with it (`hub` last used 2026-09-24; `proc://` from that same day on; 298 `proc://` calls and 503 `hub` calls in the author's session store). The grammar is `internal-urls/proc-protocol.ts`: `proc://` lists, `proc://<id>` reads status/log, `write proc://<id>` sends stdin (empty = Enter), `write proc://<id>/kill` cancels a job or stops a service, `write proc://<id>/mode` sets `persist|session|detached`. `toolPanelKind` (`tool-renderers/registry.ts`) tests `procPathOf(tool)` FIRST — before the `xd://` device and before the tool's own name — and `resolveToolKey` returns `proc`, so `write proc://x/kill` is a Proc card and not the EDIT panel (which rendered the stdin payload as "Written File Preview") while `read proc://x` is not the READ panel (which rendered the service log as file contents under a `javascript` badge).
- **One reader for every result shape, in `src/shared/lib/omp/session/proc.ts`.** Measured shapes of `details.proc`: `{action,daemon}` (stop/stdin), `{action,daemon,input}` (stdin), `{action,daemon,mode}`, `{daemon,log,terminalRows}` (a service read), `{job,log}` (a job read), `{jobs,agents,daemons}` (the listing), `{op:'cancel',jobs,cancelled}` (a job kill). The legacy `hub` tool carried the same keys at the TOP level of `details` (`{op,daemon}`, `{jobs}`, `{daemons}`), so `procViewOf` prefers the nested bag and reads through to the top level — one `ProcView` for both, which is why `hub`'s `PANEL_KINDS` row is `'proc'` and not a second panel. `procOpLabel` maps omp's action to the word a reader sees (`stop|kill` → `stop`, `read|list` → `list`), and `procRequestOp(path, toolName)` resolves the one ambiguity the path alone cannot: a bare `proc://<id>` is `stdin` on the write transport and `read` on the read transport.
- **`panels/Proc.tsx` is the body** (it replaced `Hub.tsx`, which only ever read `details.jobs`/`details.items`). It draws the daemon fact strip (state with its own tone, pid, `up`/`ran` span, exit code, restarts, persist/detached), the stdin payload a write sent, a job row, the `cancelled` outcomes a kill returned, and the listing as three groups (Services / Jobs / Agents). Terminal rows are ANSI-stripped — omp hands the log back terminal-formatted (`\u001b[0m\u001b[2;38;5;11m$\u001b[0m …`), and the panel is not a terminal. A named process is deliberately NOT echoed as raw text under its own facts (`Started kabmo: ready pid=43148 …` under `ready · pid 43148` is one sentence twice); a FAILED call still shows its reason.
- **The span a daemon reports is the span it RAN, not the time since it started.** `procUptime` ends at `exitedAt` when the daemon exited — a service that died an hour ago has not been up for an hour. Verified in the browser against a real transcript: the card read `exited · ran 16m 29s · exit 0`, and the header facts read `stop · exited · ran 16m 29s · exit 0`.
- **Facts and phrases ride the same reader.** `src/shared/lib/chat/tool/proc-facts.ts` (split out — `summary.ts` is at the size ceiling) produces the chips, and `toolSummary` guards its `read`/`write` branches with `isProc` so a `proc://` write can no longer report `1 lines` (the line count of its own stdin payload) or a `proc://` read a file size. A cancelled job is excluded from the process count — counting it said `1 process · 1 job cancelled` for a kill that found exactly one job. `describeToolActivity` returns `Stopping <id>` / `Sending input to <id>` / `Setting mode on <id>` / `Reading process <id>` / `Listing processes`, because `Reading proc://kabmo-dev/kill` describes a service stop as a file read.
- **A `bash` that launches a named service answers with `details.service` — the same snapshot, read by the same code.** 442 real results carry it (`{name, state, ready, timedOut, pid}`). `Bash.tsx` renders it as a strip above the command (name, state, pid, span, exit code, and the `read proc://<name>` it can be inspected with) and `bashServiceFacts` gives the collapsed card its chips; without either, the only thing the card said about a live dev server was its exit code. Verified in the browser on a real session: `SERVICE · tls-fixed · ready · pid 81406 · read proc://tls-fixed`.
- **A virtual URL never reaches `/api/fs/read`.** The Read panel's lazy fallback points that route at whatever path a `read` named, and `proc://kabmo-dev` is not a file: the route refused it and the panel printed `// Loaded file: proc://kabmo-dev` as if that were its contents. `isVirtualPath` (`shared/lib/fs/paths.ts`) gates the fetch; a bare `C:\x` (one colon) stays a path. Verified in the browser: zero fetches for the virtual URL, and `src/x.ts` still lazy-loads.
- **The demo datasets carry the real shapes.** `tools-session`'s `hub` sample has its measured `details`, and `virtual-devices-session` renders a `proc://` read (a ready daemon with ANSI terminal rows) and a `/kill` (the same daemon exited) from `samples/virtual-devices-proc.ts` — split out because that session file is at the size ceiling. A sample without `details` renders as a fallback and hides every panel bug this section exists to prevent.

### Dictation (composer mic button)

- **The mic button runs omp's OWN speech-to-text, not a second implementation.** omp ships STT as a TUI feature — `stt.enabled`, `stt.modelName`, `stt.submitTrigger` under Interaction → Speech — with local on-device models and no API key. It is turned on by the `app.stt.toggle` keybinding and captures the microphone of the machine running the TUI through `@oh-my-pi/pi-natives`' `AudioCapture`.
- **None of that surface reaches a web client, which is why Kabmo drives the WORKER instead.** Verified against omp 18.4.10: `stream_start` / `stream_audio` / `stream_stop` / `stream_cancel` / `transcribe` / `download` are all answered `Unknown command` over `--mode rpc` (the surface Kabmo's agent bridge uses), and no STT entry exists in `get_available_commands` — it is neither a slash command nor a tool. What omp does expose is `omp __omp_worker_stt`, and its transport is **Bun IPC, not stdio**: the worker is spawned with `stdin`/`stdout` set to `ignore`, `serialization: "advanced"`, and frames arrive on the `ipc` channel. A stdio client sees a silent, unresponsive process. The Kabmo runs on Bun, so `omp-stt-worker.ts` spawns the same worker and speaks the same frames.
- **Frames** (request/response by `id`): → `{type:'ping', id}`, `{type:'transcribe', id, modelKey, audio, language}`; ← `{type:'pong', id}`, `{type:'progress', id, event}`, `{type:'transcription', id, text}`, `{type:'error', id, error}`. `audio` is a **`Float32Array` of normalized samples at 16 kHz**, sent as structured clone over the IPC channel — NOT base64 and not an encoded file (a base64 WAV string fails with "AudioContext is not available in your environment"; a `Uint8Array` fails with "expects input to be a Float32Array"). The browser side captures PCM16, and `src/server/routes/dictation/ws.ts` converts with `pcm16ToFloat32` before `transcribe` — so the PCM16 lives on the WIRE (browser → server), not in the worker frame. omp 18.8.x also exposes a STREAMING path (`stream_start`/`stream_audio`/`stream_stop`/`stream_cancel` with `partial`/`segment` events, segmented by the worker's endpointer) which Kabmo does not use yet: it still sends one utterance per socket, which is what omp's own TUI does (its text appears on release, not per word).
- **Model keys are omp's, and the default is deliberately NOT omp's.** The four keys are `whisper-base`, `whisper-small`, `whisper-large-v3-turbo`, `parakeet-tdt-0.6b-v3`; anything else answers `Unknown stt model`. omp's own default is Parakeet v3, which covers **25 European languages only** — so Kabmo defaults to `whisper-base`, the smallest MULTILINGUAL tier (~80 MB on first use). A first run that downloads 465 MB to transcribe a non-European language wrongly is the worst possible default.
- **One utterance per socket, and the audio travels in ONE frame.** Streaming chunks while the user speaks buys nothing: omp's STT is not incremental (its own TUI inserts text on release), so a chunked transport would only add reordering and reassembly for a result the final decode replaces. The socket exists for the other direction — the server pushes model download progress while a first-run decode waits on a fetch.
- **The two progress numbers under the same field are different quantities, and neither is dependable enough to trust blindly.** `progress_total` covers the whole download while `progress` covers a single file, so only the former may set the figure — the per-file value taken as the total jumps backwards at every file boundary. But on a real first-run fetch (omp 18.4.10, ~76 MB at ~68 KB/s) the total reached 100 within the first eight seconds and then climbed past it to 2654 while the files were still arriving, because `loaded` accumulates across retries while `total` stays fixed. `summarizeProgress` therefore reports a `progress_total` outside 0..100 as INDETERMINATE (percent `null`, status stays "downloading") instead of pinning a 100 that would sit on screen for the whole download and read as "finished but stuck". The route then drops frames that do not move the percent by a point or flip the phase — a single download emits hundreds of them.
- **Capture uses `AudioContext` + `ScriptProcessorNode`.** The node is deprecated in favour of `AudioWorklet`, but it is the only capture path every target browser ships (Safari included) without serving a separate worklet module. `MediaRecorder` is not an option: it emits Opus/WebM, which the worker cannot decode.
- **The worker handle is anchored on `globalThis`** for the same reason the database and terminal store are: `bun --hot` re-evaluates the module while the spawned worker (and the model it holds in memory) lives on, and a module-level binding would orphan the child with no handle left to kill it. It shuts down after 10 minutes idle, and `/reload-engine` recycles it alongside the other omp processes.
- **The WebSocket upgrade is origin-checked** with the same predicate the terminal uses (`server/lib/http/same-origin.ts`), because dictation triggers real work — a model download and a decode — and an upgrade carries no preflight to stop a cross-origin page from asking for it.

### Chat Transcript Store (`chat_sessions`)
- **The Kabmo's copy of a conversation is an OVERLAY, not a transcript.** For a session omp owns on disk the session JSONL is authoritative (`loadSessionMessages` → `mergeOmpAttachments`), and the DB row carries only what the JSONL cannot record: the **user turns** — the text as the user typed it, since omp delivers `@agent` as a task-tool delegation prompt and `/skill:a b` as `/skill:a`, plus the attachment metadata, since a text file is inlined into the prompt and its name/content/preview exist nowhere else — and the **notice rows** (a builtin command's output, for which omp writes no entry at all). Nothing else in the row is read: `merge-stored.ts` filters on `role === 'user'` and on a non-empty `notice`, and no code path reads an assistant row.
- **Only those rows are written.** `overlayRowsForOmpSession` (`src/server/lib/omp/session/merge-stored.ts`) narrows what the POST handler stores, gated in `src/server/routes/chat/session.ts` on `findSessionFileById` — **the session file's existence, never the id's shape**: a Kabmo-created `new-<ts>` session has no file, and there the DB is the only copy, so it stays whole, as does an omp uuid whose file is gone. Before this the client posted its entire rendered timeline on every `message_end` and the server stored it verbatim — measured on the author's install at **165.6 MB of 167.9 MB of mirrored assistant rows nothing consumes**, on top of re-writing a multi-megabyte array per stream segment with no body-size guard. Verified: narrowing changed the merged timeline of **0 of 260** stored sessions compared against their own files.
- **Nothing the narrowing drops is needed to place a row.** A stored raw turn that no JSONL user turn relates to — a `/usage` or `/compact` turn, for which omp writes no entry at all — is positioned by its own `startedAt` against the merged rows' clocks (`anchorIndexFor` in `merge-stored.ts`), never by scanning the stored array for a row the JSONL carries. That scan is what made the first version of this narrowing move those turns to the END of the timeline, after answers that came later: it resolved against the mirrored rows that are exactly what gets dropped. The scan survives only as the fallback for a legacy row with no clock to read. Verified on the author's install: of the sessions holding such turns, only the ones whose anchor the scan had been supplying changed order, and they changed to the time the turn was sent. Do not "fix" the missing assistant rows — their absence is the point.
- **`compactChatOverlayRows` applies the same rule to rows written before it existed**, called from the **server bootstrap right after the "listening" banner** (`src/server/index.ts`) and deliberately NOT from `getDb()`: the test suite and the CLI reach that in real mode, so a database handle must never be able to launch a rewrite of every mirrored conversation — **a `bun test` run did exactly that, compacting a live 172 MB database**. It has **no marker**, unlike `queue_fk_removed`: the guard is per-row ("does the filter actually shrink this row?"), so the pass is self-limiting — measured 762 ms for a 173 MB overlay on the first start, then one parse-and-skip of the remaining ~5 MB — whereas a marker would leave every row written by an older build uncompacted for good, and a live server keeps writing mirrors until it is restarted. Only a row whose session file exists is rewritten, so a Kabmo-created session stays whole. The rewrite makes the freed pages reusable; `VACUUM` on a stopped server is what returns them to the filesystem.
- **Two other writers sit deliberately outside the gate**: `src/shared/lib/chat/stream-service.ts` is the MOCK SSE path, where the DB genuinely is the transcript, and `src/server/routes/chat/root.ts`'s action is the pre-migration writer that no client calls (its loader's `?sessionId=` branch likewise has no caller).

### OMP Engine Settings Schema (generated)

- **`src/client/data/settings/omp-schema.json` is GENERATED, never hand-edited.** `bun run omp:schema` (`scripts/omp-schema.mjs`) imports `orderedSettings()` from the installed `@oh-my-pi/pi-coding-agent` and `SETTING_TABS`/`TAB_METADATA` from `pi-tui`, and writes the panel's tab/group/label/editor metadata from that. `bun run omp:schema:check` regenerates in memory and exits non-zero when the file is stale, so a stale schema is a CI failure rather than a shipped panel — that gate is the `schema` job in `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, deliberately separate from `gates` because the generator imports the INSTALLED package (this repo does not depend on `@oh-my-pi/pi-coding-agent`) and a cold Bun global install would otherwise sit in front of every other check. It was **documented here before it existed**: no workflow called the script until 2026-10-12, which is how the file drifted 9 rows behind omp 18.9.2 unnoticed (now 419 entries, `10 tabs / 559 settings` at omp 18.12.6, 0 of Kabmo's 442 merged keys rejected by `omp config list --json`). The JSON sat frozen from the Remix→Elysia migration (2026-09-18) until this existed: **157 live settings had no row at all** (every `title.*`, `tui.renderSvg`, `tui.autoGraph`, `task.completionProbe`, `skills.*`, `ida.*`, `searxng.*`, `gc.*`, `memories.*`, `compaction.v2RetainedMessageBudget`, `browser.tern`, `stream.serverUrl`, `telemetry.otlpExportEnabled`), and **12 rows Kabmo still offered were rejected by `omp config get` with `Unknown setting:`** — `stt.modelName`, `providers.tts`, `tts.localModel`, `providers.webSearchOrder`, `irc.timeoutMs`, `providers.autoThinkingModel`, `providers.imageOrder`, `providers.memoryModel`, `providers.tinyModel`, `providers.unexpectedStopModel`, `providers.webSearchExclude`, `providers.webSearchGeminiModel` — an editor whose write was guaranteed to fail.
- **The generator reads omp's own source, not its bundle.** The published package ships `src/` beside `dist/`, and the definitions live in `src`; `dist/cli.js` is minified, so its `re({…})` calls only partially evaluate (the enum and `ui` shape survive, an expression-valued default does not). A setting with no `ui` block has no row in omp's own panel either, so it is deliberately not carried — and `schema-extras.ts` still supplies Kabmo's documented extras, which never shadow an upstream entry (asserted by `schema.test.ts`).
- **`task.completionProbe` replaced `task.completionProbeMs`** in omp 18.5.0 (`0` → off, any period → on), so the old key is gone from the schema and offering it would write a key the binary rejects.

### Model Roles & Presets (OMP Engine settings)

- **A role row is a MODEL PICKER, not a text field.** `modelRoles` holds a `provider/model` selector, which a user cannot type reliably and which fails silently when mistyped — omp falls back to its own resolution and the role runs on a different model than the panel shows. `RoleRow.tsx` renders a `<select>` grouped by provider (native type-ahead, keyboard and screen-reader semantics for free), and the **stored value is always an option even when the catalog no longer lists it** (a model removed from models.yml, a provider that lost its credential, a catalog read that failed): dropping it would make the select display a model config.yml does not hold, and the next edit would write that one over it.
- **The candidates come from `omp models --json --kind all`, not from `get_available_models`.** The RPC answers CHAT models only (`modelRegistry.getAvailable()` defaults to kind `chat`), so the five kind roles would have offered nothing; the CLI lists every kind (~1.3 s, cached 60 s on `globalThis`). The cache is dropped by `invalidateModelsCaches` — the same signal every provider/model mutation already emits — because a model that disappeared must leave the role pickers too. A catalog failure is REPORTED (`candidatesError`) rather than thrown: the roles live in config.yml and stay editable without it, and an empty dropdown with no explanation reads as "no models exist".
- **The role table is ONE shared module** (`src/shared/lib/omp/config/model-roles.ts`), read by the editor, the pickers and the tests. It mirrors omp's `MODEL_ROLES` at 18.12.6 — including **`widget` ("Job widgets")**, which this panel had omitted, making it the one role unreachable from the console. Each role's upstream `accepts` predicate is flattened into a catalog-kind list; `web` additionally admits a chat model declaring a grounding transport, which the CLI's JSON does not carry, so that one case is a `webCapable` flag and `roleAcceptsCandidate` is the single predicate.
- **A preset is Kabmo's own record, in `model_role_presets`** (`src/server/lib/model-roles/{schema,store}.server.ts`). omp has the same idea upstream (`modelPresets.<name>` driven by `/modelpreset`), but that surface is TUI-only and Kabmo does not own that key — a second writer on a file omp rewrites is how a config gets clobbered. The preset's whole map is one JSON column: it is applied as a UNIT, which is the only operation that exists. `name` is `COLLATE NOCASE UNIQUE`, and a collision is reported as a sentence ("A preset named “cheap” already exists") rather than the SQLite constraint text.
- **Role ids are NOT narrowed to the built-ins.** `modelRoles` accepts custom role ids and omp adds them to its own role list, so filtering a preset's keys would silently delete a custom role the moment it was applied — the map is written whole, and a filtered copy is an edit of the live config. Only keys that cannot be a role name (and empty selectors) are dropped.
- **`PUT /api/model-roles` writes the whole map; that one verb serves both a single row edit and applying a preset.** config.yml holds the map whole either way, and two writers is how a preset and a row edit end up disagreeing. The client adopts the server's echo, and a REFUSED write restores the previous map (`useModelRoles`) — leaving the optimistic draft on screen after config.yml rejected it shows an assignment that does not exist, and the next edit builds on it.
- **The selected preset and "in use" are two different facts.** `in use` is derived by comparing the live map with the preset, so there is no stored "last applied" flag to go stale. The SELECTION is not: tying it to the comparison made `Update` unreachable in the exact flow it exists for — pick a preset, tweak one role, save the tweak back into it — because the edit moved the picker to `Custom` and disabled the button. The picker therefore reads `preset · modified` while they differ, `in use` only while they agree, and `Update`/`Delete` always target the selected preset (disabled only with no selection). Delete confirms inline; `Save as…` names a new preset.
- **The first open snapshots the roles an install already had into ONE preset** (`seedPresetFromCurrentRoles`, called from the `GET` loader). A machine that has been running omp for months has a full `modelRoles` map and an empty preset table, so the picker would offer nothing until the user hand-saves what it is already running. Three cases step aside and still spend the one run: an EMPTY map (a fresh install has nothing to snapshot), a table that already holds a preset, and MOCK mode (a demo must not write rows into a real database). The name is `Default` — what it is, not a strategy claim the seeder cannot make — and the `app_settings` marker `model_role_presets_seeded` is written on EVERY deciding path, the same rule `workspace_sync_ran` follows: "the table is empty" cannot separate never-seeded from user-deleted, so without the marker a deleted preset would come back on the next panel open. Verified end to end: a temp install with four roles came back from its first `GET` with `Default` holding exactly that map, a second `GET` did not duplicate it, a `DELETE` followed by another `GET` stayed empty, and the marker read `true`. A failed insert is swallowed (the panel's own `Save as…` is the manual path) and still spends the run, so a config omp rejects cannot make every open retry.
- **The panel's role wiring lives in `useModelRoles`** (`src/client/hooks/settings/model-roles.ts`) — the load is still the panel's single `Promise.all` so one spinner covers the screen, hence `applyPayload` beside the three writes.

### Standby omp Processes (Reload OMP, omp update)

- **A reload means DISPOSE, not re-read.** `recycleStandbyProcesses` (`src/server/lib/omp/session/standby.server.ts`) disposes the two pools that hold an omp process with **no session and no turn** — the shared utility children (`--no-session --no-lsp`, answering `get_available_models`, `get_login_providers`, `get_available_commands`) and the per-cwd prewarm entries (an idle session host waiting for the next send) — so the next command boots the binary currently installed. `/reload-plugins` is a different question and lives in `reload.server.ts`: it re-reads skill/plugin FILES inside a live process and would leave the OLD BINARY running, which is exactly the staleness this exists to fix.
- **Why it is needed at all: a running child is the build it was spawned from, for its whole life.** `omp update` replaces the launcher and the package on disk, but a utility child lives up to 5 minutes (`IDLE_KILL_MS`) and a prewarm waits for a send that may be an hour away — so the provider list, the model list and the composer's `/` popup keep answering from the previous version with nothing on screen to say so. Verified end to end against a real server: after a successful update the utility child's pid was gone and the next `/api/models` booted a new one, while an **already up-to-date** run left the same pid alive (`applyOmpUpdate` returns `updated: false` for the phrase `checkOmpUpdate` already matches — a success that must not bounce a warm pool).
- **Live sessions are deliberately untouched.** They may be mid-turn, and a `--resume` on their next command already boots the current binary. The button is therefore safe to press at any time, and the smoke run proves it: with a real session host and a utility child alive, `POST /api/omp/reload-engine` recycled the utility pool and left the session's pid running and its `get_state` answering.
- **The prewarm pool's recycle is claim-safe by construction.** An unclaimed entry owns no session and no turn, and `startNewRpcSession` claims an entry with a synchronous check-and-set — so an entry is either taken before the pass reads it or taken after the pass removed it, never both. `restartPrewarmedSessions` (`rpc/session-registry.ts`) marks each doomed entry claimed before deleting it, then awaits `destroyAndWait`; a wrapper still booting is torn down the same way the prewarm's own error path does.
- **A resumed session can come back under a DIFFERENT id, and the registry must answer for the one the CLIENT holds.** omp 18.10.0 forks a session marked moved away (`omp session moved`, or a `departed` `session-transfer` marker) instead of continuing it: the child answers a fresh `sessionId` and writes the fork to a fresh file under a fresh id — verified against a real child, which resumed a departed file into another directory. The registry is keyed by the id the caller holds (the URL's session id), so keying it on that alone MISSED on the very next message: the send re-resolved the ORIGINAL file and spawned again, and **two sends on one session file produced two children with two different ids**, each forking its own file — a chain of forks with a sidebar row each and nothing on screen to explain it (measured through both `startRpcSession` and the route-level lookup). `registerSpawnedSession` (`rpc/session-registration.ts`) therefore registers the wrapper under the reported id AND, when they differ, under the requested one, removing both on destroy so a dead child cannot answer for the requested id; the spawn path also emits `session-attached` for the requested id so a subscribed tab re-snapshots instead of holding the pre-spawn `running:false`. `realSessionId` reports the child's OWN id even on a registry hit (it reported the requested id, which is the alias, not the session). Pinned by `forked-resume.test.ts`, which drives the real spawn path against `test-support/fake-omp-fork.ts` — the only way the two ids can differ — and fails with 2 children if the alias is removed.
- **Caches built FROM a process or the binary are dropped in the same pass** (`invalidateModelsCaches`, `invalidateSkillCache`, `invalidateUsageCache`, `invalidateOmpCliCache`), because a recycled server behind a cached provider/model list makes the recycle look like it did nothing. The client half is `useReloadOmpEngine` (`src/client/hooks/settings/reload-engine.ts`), which also calls `invalidateComposerCache` + `notifyModelsUpdated` — the `/` popup holds its list for 5 minutes and would otherwise keep offering the old commands.
- **Two triggers, one pass.** `POST /api/omp/reload-engine` (route `src/server/routes/omp/reload-engine.ts`) is the settings sidebar's "Reload OMP" button — previously a 1200 ms spinner with no request behind it — and `applyUpdate`'s omp branch fires the same recycle after a successful update, gated on `updated` so an already-current run does not bounce anything. Both coalesce onto one in-flight pass, and a failure in one pool never skips the other (each is caught and reported separately; `standby.server.test.ts` pins all four properties).
- **The toast carries a tone.** The settings modal's single toast slot now reports the reload's real outcome — `Reloaded omp engine — N utility, M prewarmed process(es) recycled.`, or the empty-pool wording when nothing was live (a normal state, not a failure: the next command spawns the current build either way) — and a failed request renders under an alert icon in `text-error` instead of a check mark. Verified in the browser on a live server: the click issued `POST /api/omp/reload-engine → 200`, the toast named `1 utility, 0 prewarmed`, and a forced 500 rendered `alert-circle text-error`.

### Updates from the Console (self-restart and the page reload)

- **A Kabmo update from the console restarts the server that answered it, and the page then reloads ITSELF into the new build.** `POST /api/updates/apply` arms the detached restart and answers first (`RESTART_GRACE_MS`, 1.2 s — the margin that lets the `result` frame reach the browser), so the page is still served by the process whose files were just replaced. Reloading on the `result` frame would fetch the OLD bundle back; never reloading leaves the user on a stale page with a "restarting" toast and nothing else. `useUpdates` therefore reads `/api/health` BEFORE the run, waits for a process that answers with a different `pid`/`startedAt`, and only then reloads (`src/client/hooks/ui/updates/restart.ts`, `waitForServerRestart`).
- **Identity, never a timer, is what "the replacement is up" means.** A timer is wrong in both directions: too short reloads into the dying process, too long leaves the user on a stale page after the new one is already serving. `/api/health` reports the serving process's `pid` and `startedAt`, so a different pair IS the replacement. A failed probe is EXPECTED (the old server is exiting and the port is briefly unbound) and is never read as an answer; with no baseline — the pre-run health read failed — the fallback is a young `uptime` (`REPLACEMENT_MAX_UPTIME_S`), which the update's own multi-minute duration makes safe. The ceiling is 30 s (`RESTART_WAIT_TIMEOUT_MS`), and on a timeout the result comes back with `restarting: false` and a sentence telling the user to reload by hand rather than a promise the console cannot keep. Verified against a live server: the real `/api/health` payload carries both fields, a re-read of the same process is not a replacement, and a changed pid is.
- **`restarting` is a FIELD on the result, not something the client sniffs out of the message.** `UpdateApplyResult.restarting` is set from the plan `scheduleSelfRestart` returned, and the sentence is folded in by the same helper (`withRestartPlan`, `src/server/lib/lifecycle/restart.ts`) so the flag and the wording cannot drift apart. Absent on every other outcome — an "already up to date" success, a `manual` install, a failure — and `false` when a restart was refused (an instance the CLI does not own), where the appended sentence is the only place the user learns why.
- **The message was wrong, and this is the fix.** It read `… — the console reconnects on its own`, which described the realtime socket and not the page: the console stayed on the old bundle until the user reloaded by hand. It now says `Restarting on port <port> to apply it — this page reloads on its own.`, pinned by `selfRestartMessage`'s own test.
- **The dialog stays in its "working" state across the wait, and the install log is APPENDED to rather than replaced.** A run that flashed back to idle before the navigation would read as finished; `apply` therefore leaves `applying` set when it ends by navigating, and appends `Update installed — waiting for the server to restart on this port…` to the log it already has, because a failed restart has to be read against the output that ran. An abort (the panel going away with the run) still returns `null` — the same answer the other abort path gives — so no caller toasts into a torn-down view.
- **The omp target never enters this path.** `applyUpdate`'s omp branch recycles the standby pools and restarts nothing, so the client only reads the baseline and only waits for `target === 'kabmo'`. `restarting` is never set for it.
- **The settings sidebar carries the same restart as a button, beside "Reload OMP", and it reuses that contract rather than a second one.** `POST /api/console/restart` (`src/server/routes/console/restart.ts`, registered by `routes/console/index.ts`; the pre-rename `/api/chamber/restart` stays mounted by `routes/chamber/index.ts`) calls `scheduleSelfRestart('restart')` — the same detached `kabmo restart --port <port>` the update path arms, on the same `RESTART_GRACE_MS` — and answers `{ success, restarting, message }`. `useRestartKabmo` (`src/client/hooks/settings/restart-kabmo.ts`) reads `/api/health` before the request, then waits through `waitForServerRestart` and reloads into the replacement, so the page cannot be left on the dying process's bundle. **The purpose argument is the whole wording difference**: an update says `Restarting on port <port> to apply it — …` and its refusal says `start it again to apply the update`, while a plain restart drops that clause (`Restarting on port <port> — …`) because there is no update to apply — one function per sentence (`selfRestartMessage`, `skipRestartNote`) with `SelfRestartPurpose` choosing the branch, so the two surfaces cannot drift. An instance the CLI does not own is REFUSED with that sentence and `restarting: false` (verified against a real `bun run src/server/index.ts`: `this instance runs from source … — start it again`), MOCK mode refuses outright because a demo must not take a server down, and the armed branch was verified end to end against a `--launch-mode=daemon` instance: `pid 76144` answered the request, and 6 s later `pid 76188` was serving the same port. Both buttons are disabled while either operation runs — a reload while the server is restarting answers a socket nobody serves.
- **A restart launches the copy the update INSTALLED, never the one the running process happens to be in — and a rename is why.** `bun add -g @kabmo-labs/kabmo` installs a SECOND package beside the one that answered the update, so resolving the helper from that process's own `PKG_ROOT` relaunches the build the update just replaced. Measured on the 4.23.1 → 0.0.1 move: the log read `[ompchamber] restarting on port 3001: … /node_modules/ompchamber/src/cli/ompchamber.js restart --port 3001`, the console came back as **4.23.1**, and the update had reported success. `updateKabmo` therefore reads the copy back off disk AFTER its steps (`installed-cli.ts`: `copyAt` for a checkout, `resolveInstalledCopy` for a global install) and returns it on `KabmoUpdateResult.installed` → `AppliedUpdate.installed` → `scheduleSelfRestart('update', applied.installed)`; `kabmo update` does the same through `restartCtx`. A null falls back to this process's own root, which is correct for `Restart` (it installs nothing) and for a copy whose install could not be read.
- **The CLI file comes from the package's own `bin` map, and the credential marker follows the TARGET package.** Two consequences of a rename that fail silently on their own. A hard-coded `src/cli/kabmo.js` does not exist in a copy that names its bin differently, so `restartInvocation` resolves the bin from `package.json`, preferring the entry named after the package (`@kabmo-labs/kabmo` → `kabmo`) because a bridge release registers both and picking the first key would restart a renamed install through the legacy spelling. And the handover variable was renamed too (`OMPCHAMBER_PASSWORD_HANDOVER` → `KABMO_PASSWORD_HANDOVER`), so a restart that set only its own spelling handed a payload to a CLI that never reads it and the replacement came back with **authentication disabled** — `HANDOVER_ENV_BY_PACKAGE` keys the marker by the target's package name. Both are pinned by `installed-cli.test.ts` (including that a pre-rename copy keeps the old marker) and `update.test.js` (`restartCtx` moves the spawn to the installed root, and leaves it alone when the update reported none).
- **What is not covered by a test:** the real self-restart followed by a browser reload. Exercising it means killing a live CLI-managed instance mid-session, so the wiring is pinned by the pieces (`withRestartPlan` sets the flag and the sentence together; the hook's mounts drive the wait, the reload and the timeout through an injected clock and a stubbed `window.location`; the health payload is smoke-tested against a live server) and the assembled path is not.

### Plugins (Settings → Plugins)

- **The panel is a view over omp's own plugin state, and every mutation is `omp plugin …`.** omp owns four files — `<dataRoot>/marketplaces.json`, `<scope>/plugins/installed_plugins.json`, `<scope>/plugins/omp-plugins.lock.json` and the shared `cache/{marketplaces,plugins}/` — and re-implementing a write whose format omp also reads at runtime would be a second source of truth for state omp reports. The Kabmo's own job is the two things the CLI cannot answer: WHICH features and settings a plugin declares (its manifest) and WHERE its runtime state lives (the lockfile of its scope). `src/server/lib/omp/config/{plugin-registry,plugins,plugin-cli,plugin-actions}.ts`, route `src/server/routes/settings/plugins.ts`, UI `src/client/components/settings/categories/plugin-settings/`.
- **A plugin id is not unique — the selection is `(id, scope)`.** The same `name@marketplace` installs into BOTH registries at once, and omp lists the user copy with `shadowedBy: "project"` while the project copy shadows it; the two differ in version, enablement, install path and shadowing. Selecting by id made them one row, so the pane could render the user copy and act on the project copy. `pluginSelectionKey` (`plugin-settings/selection.ts`) is the key the rows, the selection and every action name.
- **`cwd` is the scope selector, not a filter.** `omp plugin` reads the PROJECT registry of the directory it runs in, so a workspace scope lists that workspace's project installs beside the user ones and the user scope runs in `$HOME` — which omp's own anchor walk deliberately stops before, because `~/.omp` is the USER config dir. A project install issued from a user-scope cwd would land in the wrong registry. `getProjectPluginsDir` (`core/paths.ts`) is a faithful port of omp's `resolveOrDefaultProjectRegistryPath` (nearest `.omp/`, else nearest `.git`, else the cwd).
- **The user plugin root is the DATA root, not `~/.omp`.** `getPluginsDir`/`getMarketplacesRegistryPath` follow omp's `DirResolver` `data` category, so on an XDG install they are `$XDG_DATA_HOME/omp/…`. Reading `~/.omp/plugins` unconditionally reports an empty plugin list there — the same trap `agentDataSubdir` documents for sessions. The condition is omp's own: the XDG app root must already EXIST.
- **A marketplace entry carries no manifest, and its runtime state is keyed by the PACKAGE NAME.** `omp plugin list --json` reports `{ id, scope, entries }` for a marketplace install, with no features and no settings; both live in the cached install directory, and its lockfile state sits under `@local/manifest-plugin`, never `manifest-plugin@local-mkt`. Reading the id against the lockfile reports "no features" for every marketplace plugin (measured), which is why `readPluginPackageName` exists.
- **Features are saved as ONE selection through `--set`, not as `--enable`/`--disable`.** The add/remove pair cannot express "no optional features", so a plugin that started with features on could never be returned to omp's defaults. `selected === null` means omp is applying the features marked `default`, and that is what the draft starts from. **A project-scoped install's features are read-only**, because omp's `features` command resolves the plugin from its USER plugin root (`getPluginsNodeModules()`) and writes the USER lockfile: on a project-shadowed pair it reports success while the ACTIVE project selection is untouched (verified on 18.4.4 — `features --set alpha` wrote `~/.omp/plugins/omp-plugins.lock.json` and left the project lock's `enabledFeatures` null). `featuresEditable`/`featuresNote` carry that to the pane, which renders the ladder without its save controls, and the route refuses the same request so a stale client cannot silently write the shadowed copy.
- **A plugin's SETTINGS live in the user lockfile in both scopes.** `omp plugin config set/delete` go through `PluginManager`'s single runtime config (`getPluginsLockfile()`) and have no `--scope` at all — the runtime reads them back the same way (`getPluginSettings(name, cwd)` merges that global map with the project overrides file). Reading the scope's own lockfile reports "no settings" for every project-scoped install (verified: a `config set` issued from a workspace cwd wrote the user lock and left the project lock's `settings` empty), so `listPlugins` reads values from the user lock in both scopes while `enable`/`disable` state still comes from the scope's own lock.
- **Features and settings are addressed by PACKAGE NAME, not by plugin id.** omp's `features` and `config` commands resolve the plugin from its `node_modules` entry, so a marketplace plugin needs `@local/manifest-plugin` — a bare catalog name exits 1 with `Plugin "<name>" not found` (measured). `PluginItem.packageName` carries it; `name` stays the catalog name the user sees.
- **A secret setting's value never leaves the server.** `omp-plugins.lock.json` stores plugin settings in plaintext and the pane is a browser surface, so `pluginSettingState` reports a `secret: true` key as present (`secretSet`) and omits its value — the pane can replace or clear it, never show it, which is also omp's own answer (`config list` masks it). The input starts EMPTY rather than prefilled with a placeholder a careless Save would write back literally.
- **`--json` is not honoured by every `omp plugin` verb.** install, `marketplace add/remove/update` and upgrade ignore it and answer on stdout as text, reporting failure on stderr with exit code 1 — so `runPluginCli` returns both streams and `pluginCliError` prefers stderr. A caller reading stdout alone reports every failure as an empty success. omp's own sentence is surfaced verbatim ("Plugin \"nope\" not found in marketplace …", "is installed in both user and project scope"), because it names the missing marketplace or the ambiguous scope, neither of which Kabmo could reconstruct.
- **Every mutation answers with the whole panel payload, and the server re-reads omp's registries.** Installing moves the catalog's `installed` flag and can add a lockfile entry; uninstalling can drop a cache directory another scope referenced; an upgrade changes the version a second scope sees. Adopting the response verbatim is what keeps one source of truth.
- **A scope flag rides along only when it can matter.** The uninstall/enable actions send `--scope` for a marketplace plugin (whose id can exist in both registries), and nothing for a `package` plugin (npm/git/link), which omp resolves by name against the active project root first and the user root second — the flag is only honoured on the marketplace path. Features and settings carry the panel's scope instead, because that is the cwd those two commands resolve in.
- **`reloadLiveSessions()` runs after every accepted mutation.** omp discovers plugin capabilities once per process, so a newly installed plugin's skills and commands are invisible to a running session until `/reload-plugins` — the same refresh the Skills panel performs after a SKILL.md write.
- **MOCK mode answers a deterministic four-plugin dataset** (`src/client/data/settings/plugin.ts`) covering every render shape: a marketplace plugin, a project install with an update available, a shadowed disabled user copy, and a plugin with no `omp` manifest at all (conventional `skills/` content only).

### Skills (Settings → Skills, composer `/` popup)

- **Skill discovery delegates to omp, it is not re-implemented here.** `discoverSkills` (`src/server/lib/omp/config/skills.ts`) runs `omp skill list <dir> --json` — omp's own resolver, so it reports exactly what a session in that directory would load. A hand-rolled scan of `~/.omp/agent/skills` + `<cwd>/.omp/skills` is what it replaced, and it silently disagreed with the agent in both directions: it listed 6 skills where omp loaded 9, and it could not see `~/.agents/skills` (where `bunx skills add --agent universal` installs), `.claude`, `.codex`, `.github`, `.opencode`, plugin packages or the skillshare store. It also re-derived frontmatter rules it could not keep in sync — a missing `description` makes omp skip a SKILL.md entirely, and `hide`/`disableModelInvocation` keeps a skill runnable but out of the model's `<skills>` listing. One CLI call (~0.4 s, no session, no model) answers all of it, cached 5 s per directory (`invalidateSkillCache` after every write).
- **A skill is only editable where Kabmo is the owner.** `managedSkillRoots` is `~/.omp/agent/skills` plus `<workspace>/.omp/skills`, and `managed` is true only for an `native:*` skill sitting directly under one of them. Everything else — a registry package in `~/.agents/skills`, a Claude plugin — renders read-only and its `DELETE` is refused by name, because Kabmo is not the tool that put it there. The detail pane shows the source path instead of an editor it must not save through.
- **Create/save/delete are real SKILL.md files** (`writeSkillFile` / `deleteSkillDir`), not rows in an `app_settings` list. The old "custom skill" wrote only to Kabmo's own store and omp never read it: the panel offered a save that changed nothing, and deleting it removed a record while the skill (or nothing at all) stayed as it was. A rename or a scope move (`user` ⇄ `project`) removes the previous directory, which is why `writeSkillFile` takes `previousDir` rather than a name — resolving the old path from the new scope silently left the source copy behind (verified: a user→project move left both).
- **The catalog install is scoped to the row that was clicked.** `installCatalogSkill` passes `--skill <name>`; without it `bunx skills add <repo>` installs every skill in the repository (measured: 20 directories for one "Install"). Uninstall runs the CLI's `skills remove` counterpart, so it clears what the install created instead of only Kabmo's record.
- **omp discovers skills once per process, so a write is followed by a reload — and the reload reaches EVERY live process, including a busy one.** A SKILL.md that lands while a session runs is invisible to that session until it restarts (measured: `get_available_commands` kept reporting 8 entries after a ninth was written). `reloadLiveSessions` (`src/server/lib/omp/session/reload.server.ts`) sends `/reload-plugins` — omp's only refresh, reachable as a prompt because the builtin has a text-mode `handle` — and it broadcasts to the per-session children AND the pooled utility processes (which answer the composer's `/` popup). **The `isBusy()` skip is gone, and its premise was wrong.** The old guard reasoned that a prompt dispatched into a running turn is a queue insert; measured on omp 18.4.3, it is not. A `/reload-plugins` sent mid-turn is answered from omp's command loop in 16-40 ms with the turn still streaming — and with a blocking approval dialog parked (`get_state`, `get_available_commands` and the reload all answered within 16 ms; the RPC loop is independent of the turn waiting on the dialog) — after which the turn completes normally and the new skill is listed immediately. A PLAIN mid-turn prompt is neither queued nor injected: it is dropped outright (one `agent_start` for two prompts). The guard was actively harmful, because the common case IS a busy session: `create-skill` writes its SKILL.md with a tool WHILE the invoking chat streams, so the write landed during a run and the refresh was skipped — leaving the new skill invisible until the user found the manual `⟳` button (verified against a live Kabmo: absent from `get_available_commands` after the turn ended, present the moment a session reloaded). Verified on 18.4.3 it answers `agentInvoked: false` (no model turn) and leaves the session file byte-identical, and a burst of triggers coalesces onto one in-flight pass. Verified end to end: creating a skill through the API took a live session 9 → 10 skill entries with no restart, deleting it took the session back to 9, and a hand-written skill mid-turn was listed while the turn still streamed.
- **A SKILL.md written by anything OTHER than Kabmo is watched for, not polled.** The root set is ONE table, `src/server/lib/omp/config/discovery-roots.ts`, shared by `locateCommandFile` and the watcher — they were written separately and the watcher's copy was short by every root but the omp-native pair, so a skill added to `.claude/skills`, `.codex/skills`, `.opencode/skills`, `.agents/skills` or `.github/skills` in a live session was never noticed. The set is measured against omp 18.4.3, not assumed: the project roots are those six, the user roots are `~/.omp/agent/skills` and `~/.agents/skills`, and `~/.claude/skills`, `~/.codex/skills`, `~/.config/opencode/skills` and `~/.opencode/skills` were verified NOT read — do not add a root on the strength of its command counterpart (omp does read `~/.claude/commands` and friends). Skills and commands share the project roots; `.github` differs by layout (`.github/prompts/*.prompt.md` for commands) and `~/.agents/skills` is skills-only. `src/server/lib/omp/config/roots-watch.server.ts` watches those roots for the user scope, for each registered workspace, and for each LIVE session — a session can run outside every registered workspace (a folder opened straight from the sidebar, the app root) and omp still resolves project skills from that cwd (verified: without this, a SKILL.md written into such a session's own `.omp/skills` was never picked up). It refreshes every live child 250 ms after a change (one reload per burst). The alternative — re-running `omp skill list` on a timer per workspace — spawns a process per tick to answer a question that only changes when a file does. Three rules make it work. **A root that does not exist is never created** (Kabmo must not write `.omp/skills` into a user's repository just to watch it): the nearest existing ancestor is watched instead, and the change that creates the real root triggers a re-sync, which re-attaches it RECURSIVELY. **omp also reads project skills from every ANCESTOR of the cwd, up to the enclosing git repository** — measured on 18.4.3: from `/tmp/walk/a/b/c` with no `.git` anywhere it reported the skills of all four levels, an empty `.git` at `a/b` cut it to that level and below, a `.git` FILE (worktree/submodule) stops it the same way, and `$HOME` is NOT a boundary (the walk crossed it, and `~/.omp/skills` was never read). `skillRoots()` therefore appends `<ancestor>/.omp/skills` for each level, `managed: false` so Kabmo's write/delete paths stay scoped to the cwd's own root. Without this, a skill written into the ancestor a session actually resolves was invisible (verified: a session in `/tmp/anc/sub` saw `a-parent` from `/tmp/anc/.omp/skills` but never `a-late` added later). Only a genuine root is watched recursively, and a BRIDGE is watched not at all in the sense that matters: the nearest existing ancestor of a missing root can be `~/.omp/agent` or a whole workspace, so a bridge firing only re-syncs — it never reports a change itself. Reporting those as reloads produced 170 broadcasts in 20 s on an idle machine and, worse, MASKED the ancestor gap: the skill appeared to be picked up by the watcher when it was only being re-read by a broadcast an unrelated file had triggered. Two further rules close timing holes a filesystem watch cannot: a root attached for the first time owes one delayed pass (the OS registers a recursive watch ASYNCHRONOUSLY, so a file written in the instant after a session spawn raises no event — verified: the skill was missed, and picked up once the attach settled), and a pending reload is never downgraded by a bridge pass in the same debounce window (the reload a root attach scheduled was overwritten by an unrelated directory's traffic before it ran). And the reconcile is idempotent and serialized, because `bun --hot` re-runs the server entry: a duplicated watcher would double every broadcast. The watcher set itself is anchored on `globalThis` for the reason the database is — a `bun --hot` module re-evaluation would otherwise orphan the previous `fs.watch` handles with no way to close them (confirmed: with the binding anchored in the module, a hand write stopped reaching the session after a hot reload; anchored on `globalThis`, it survived). It is reconciled at boot and on every workspace create/delete (`POST /api/folders`, `.../delete`), so a workspace added mid-session is watched without a restart. `fs.watch({recursive:true})` costs no descriptors on Bun/macOS (measured with the repo's own `countOpenFileDescriptors`: zero delta for three recursive watchers over 42 entries).
- **Both panels and the composer are scoped to one omp scope at a time.** `resolveDiscoveryScope` (`src/server/lib/omp/config/scope.ts`) turns the request into a cwd: `?scope=user` asks the agent dir (`~/.omp/agent`), `?root=<workspace>` asks that workspace, and neither keeps the historical app-root meaning (the composer's fallback for a session with no workspace folder, where omp itself spawns in the server's cwd). `resolveRoot` still gates a client-supplied `?root=` (app root, a path under it, or an exact registered `project_path`), and an unresolvable root falls back to the USER scope rather than to a directory the caller never asked about. The cwd is the whole point: omp resolves project skills and commands from the process cwd — a child in `$HOME` reported 7 skill entries and one `file` command, the same child in this repo reported 9 and its own `.omp/commands` — which is also why the utility RPC is pooled **per cwd** (`runUtilityCommand(command, timeout, cwd)`), so a home-scoped pool entry serves the registry queries while a workspace read gets its own. The composer's cache is keyed `command::<root>` and `invalidateComposerCache` matches that prefix, so `available_commands_update` still clears every workspace's copy.
- **A write lands in the scope the panel is showing, and a new draft inherits it.** The save carries the scope (`scope`/`root` on the POST body) and the draft's own `location` picks the root, so a create made while a workspace is selected writes `<workspace>/.omp/skills`; the panel passes `defaultLocation` for that, because a new draft used to open as `user` and a project skill created from the workspace scope was written to `~/.omp/agent/skills` — it then vanished from the list being viewed. A project write with no workspace is refused server-side rather than landing somewhere the user did not pick, and only the omp-native roots are writable at all (`managedSkillRoots`, `locateCommandFile`'s `managed` flag): everything else renders read-only, keeps its Save hidden, and its `DELETE` is refused by name.
- **The commands panel reads omp's own list, and finds the file behind a name itself.** `get_available_commands` reports the name, description and hint but never a path, so `withCommandFile` → `locateCommandFile` looks the name up in every root omp reads (`.omp/commands`, `.claude`, `.codex`, `.agents`, `.opencode`, `.github/prompts` — the last with its `.prompt.md` suffix); a name with no file is a builtin, a `skill:<name>` entry or an extension command and is `isBuiltIn: true, managed: false`. Only `<workspace>/.omp/commands` and `~/.omp/agent/commands` are `managed`. A name present in both roots resolves to the PROJECT file, which is what omp runs (measured), so the lookup checks the workspace first and a delete can only reach the two managed roots.
- **The panels' project picker lists real workspace folders** (`useWorkspaceRoots` → `/api/settings/projects`), not the hardcoded `kabmo`/`workspace-edge` placeholders it used to offer: those sent a root the server could not resolve, so choosing one changed nothing. Each option's value is the folder's `project_path`, which is what the API takes as `?root=`, and `rootFor` resolves an option by **id OR value** — the dropdown emits the value through `onChange`, so an id-only lookup silently answered the user scope for every workspace while the trigger still printed the folder's name. Both the Skills and the Commands panel share the hook, the trigger-label resolver and the scope query.

### Side Questions (BTW panel)
- `/btw [question]` typed in the main composer **replaces the composer with the side-question panel** (`src/client/components/workspace/btw-panel/`, swapped in by `ComposerDock`, opened by the `omp:btw` window event; the mode state machine is `src/client/hooks/chat/btw/mode.ts`); bare `/btw` opens it on the current history. **The open flag is deliberately NOT persisted** (`useState`, not `useSessionState`): a panel that reopened itself on load left the user with no chat composer and a field they never asked for, and a `/btw` typed into that field became a real question to the model. The panel is a mode the user enters, so every load starts in the chat — while the drafts and the selected topic do persist (`chat.btwAsk` / `chat.btwFollowUp` / `chat.btwTopicId`), so leaving and re-entering loses nothing. The panel is **two cards**: the panel card — the ask card as its **header** (the field that starts a new topic, plus `⟳` start over, `⌄` topic history, `⧉` promote into chat, `✕` leave), the running turn's `GeneratingIndicator`, and the topic's turns as its body — and the composer below it for follow-ups. The ask field must stay a header rather than a free-standing card above the composer: as its own card it read as a second composer and users typed follow-ups into it. The answer card is capped at `520px` (`ANSWERS_MAX_HEIGHT_PX`) with its own scrollbar, and the header stays visible while the body scrolls.
- **The panel renders the CHAT's timeline, not a btw-specific one.** A turn's `messages` are `ChatMessageData` — the same shape the chat timeline folds — so `BtwMessages` builds one row list (each question as a user row, then that turn's conversation) and hands it to the chat's own `MessageList`. A tool the side child runs therefore draws the same card it would in the chat, and thinking / usage / errors come along; the two renderers cannot drift. On the server the conversation is accumulated by `trackTurnMessage` (`src/server/lib/btw/lifecycle.server.ts`) through the shared `toChatMessage` mapper, with the SAME by-id upsert the chat stream's `onMessageUpdate` performs (omp emits one frame per segment carrying that segment's full accumulated content). `BtwTurn.answer` stays as the plain-text summary (history labels, promotion titles) and is the fallback for a turn stored before the conversation column existed.
- **The run footer's token counts come from `message_end`, not the streaming frames.** omp's `message_update` frames report `usage` zeros; only the completed message carries the real counts, so `handleFrame` upserts `message_end` too — as a COMPLETED row (`toChatMessage(record, false)`, the same flag the chat stream passes), because mapping it as streaming left `thinking.isGenerating` true on every stored turn: each reload reopened the accordion and pulsed it over a finished answer. Dropping that branch is silent: the footer renders without its `… tok` segment. The row's `thinkingLevel` comes from the chat's transcript (`loadSessionThinkingSelector`), not from `get_state` — that reports the level the child RESOLVED, and storing it would pin an `auto` chat to a concrete effort on every later spawn.
- **`BtwState.live` carries the running turn**, not just frames: a client attaching mid-turn (a reload, a second tab) would otherwise get a snapshot whose running turn has no persisted messages yet and would show an empty answer while the child streams on. The `btw_message` / `btw_activity` frames drive the live upsert; the state is the seed. The child echoes the `<btw>`-wrapped prompt it received, and the panel drops that user echo (it already renders the user's own question).
- **The indicator's verb is the side child's own.** `btw_activity` carries it, derived server-side from that child's frames (`describeAssistantPhase` / `describeToolActivity`) — the chat's verb describes a different process and must never be borrowed. `PHASE_VERBS.sideQuestion` ("Answering side question") is the honest fallback before the child reports anything, since the side stream cannot tell reasoning from prose.
- **The child ECHOES the prompt it received**, and that echo is the `<btw>` wrapper the panel itself built, so it is filtered out of the rendered rows — the panel draws the user's own question instead. A legacy turn's plain `answer` is rendered only when it has no stored conversation.
- **The panel has NO model / thinking / access pickers.** A side question runs on the CHAT's model and thinking selector, and every `ask` re-reads both from the parent transcript (`loadSessionModel` / `loadSessionThinkingSelector`) — a chat that switched model or level is mirrored by the next question, and a live child is re-targeted over RPC before the prompt (`BtwRuntime.retarget`). That is omp's own shape: its side turn reads `request.session.model` at run time, so the TUI panel has no pickers either, and Kabmo's per-topic `set_model` / `set_thinking_level` / `set_access_mode` actions are gone. `showModel`/`showThinking`/`showAccess` on `ComposerToolbar` (all off for `BtwForm`) is what keeps the composer's own selectors out of the side-question card without forking `ChatInput`.
- **omp's `/btw` is TUI-only**, so Kabmo implements it. Its registry entry in `omp 18.2.6` has `handleTui` and no text-mode `handle`, and RPC dispatch only runs entries with `handle` — sending `/btw …` as a prompt would reach the model as literal text. Client interception lives in `src/client/hooks/chat/timeline/actions.ts`; server behaviour in `src/server/lib/btw/`. Because `get_available_commands` cannot advertise it, the composer's `/` popup gets its entry from Kabmo-owned pool in `src/shared/lib/chat/composer/client.ts` (`CHAMBER_COMMANDS`, byte-identical to omp's own description and `[question]` hint) — never from omp's list.
- **Context arrives by transcript reuse, never by re-prompting it as text.** A topic owns a private copy of the parent session file (`<db dir>/btw/<sessionId>/<topicId>/session.jsonl`) taken on its first question; the side child is spawned as `omp --mode rpc-ui --resume <that copy> --no-title --no-tools` (`src/server/lib/btw/child.server.ts` builds the argv). **This is the one parity gap that cannot be closed**: omp's side turn runs IN PROCESS over the live `this.messages` (including a message still streaming), while Kabmo resumes a file snapshot — there is no RPC for an ephemeral turn, so a side question here sees the parent as of the snapshot plus its own turns, not the parent's in-flight work. The parent's prompt-cache prefix survives, the parent file is never written (verified: byte-identical after many questions), and a follow-up after the child was reclaimed resumes the copy and still sees every earlier turn.
- **The side child runs WITHOUT tools (`--no-tools`), which is what omp's `/btw` effectively is.** omp's TUI does not pass `--no-tools` (that is a CLI flag for a whole session) — it runs the side turn through `runEphemeralTurn`, which keeps the tool catalog attached only to hold the prompt cache warm, tells the model in a `<system-reminder>` that tools are NOT available, and DISCARDS any tool call the model emits anyway (`agent-session.ts` filters `toolCall` blocks out of the final message). `--no-tools` reaches the same end state from the CLI: no catalog, so nothing can execute. Verified against omp 18.3.2 on a resumed transcript that already carries 384 tool results: `toolNames` empty, zero tool calls emitted, zero executions, the turn completed normally. There is therefore no approval gate to park on and no dialog path: `PendingUiDialogs`, `BtwState.dialogs`, `ExtensionDialog` in the panel, and the `dialog_response` action are all gone. `withoutToolCalls` in `lifecycle.server.ts` drops a `toolCall` block that arrives anyway (a hallucinated call against the catalog omp would still attach), mirroring omp's own filter.
- **One side question in flight per session** — no implicit queue, no implicit cancel; a second question is refused (`btw_busy`, 409) exactly like the TUI. A new question is its own topic; only the panel's composer continues the active one.
- **State and streaming**: `btw_topics`/`btw_turns` in Kabmo DB (schema in `src/server/lib/btw/schema.server.ts`; `thinking_level`/`approval_mode` are added by the additive column migration there, because `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` leaves an existing table alone — `approval_mode` is retained for rows written while a side child ran with tools and nothing writes it now, and `thinking_level` holds the chat's SELECTOR, `auto` included). A turn's status vocabulary is omp's own (`running`/`complete`/`cancelled`/`error`/`interrupted`; a row written as `failed` is migrated by `ensureBtwSchema`), and the stored `answer` summary is bounded the way omp bounds its side replies (`src/server/lib/btw/reply.ts`). The server is the source of truth and every frame is a snapshot (`btw_state`, carrying the live turn) or a delta (`btw_message` / `btw_activity`). Commands: `POST /api/btw/:sessionId` (`ask`/`abort`/`promote`/`delete`), stream: `GET /api/btw/:sessionId/events` + WS `/:sessionId/ws`. Unlike the agent stream this one is NOT observer-only: history must be readable while no omp child is running, so reading the state also repairs `running` rows whose runtime is gone.
- **Promote** materializes the topic as a session of the chat's own: the copy is written beside the parent under a fresh id, carrying `parentSession` and a title slot padded to exactly the `SESSION_TITLE_SLOT_BYTES - 1` bytes omp rewrites in place, with the `<btw>` boilerplate unwrapped so the promoted chat shows the question the user actually asked. It is refused while a turn runs, while the CHAT is streaming, for a topic with follow-ups (`btw_multi_turn` — omp's branch carries one question/answer pair and refuses multi-turn history), and once the parent transcript has moved past the snapshot's leaf (omp's own `/btw` branch guard). **That leaf is written where the snapshot is taken** (`BtwRuntime.openWorkspace` → `setBtwTopicLeaf`); it had lost its writer in the tools change, so `readBtwTopicLeaf` always returned null and the guard could never fire — rows created before the fix keep a null leaf and are not guarded, since their snapshot entry cannot be reconstructed.

### Scheduled Tasks (sidebar Calendar button)

- **The Kabmo owns the clock, because omp has none.** `src/server/lib/schedule/runtime.server.ts` runs ONE process-wide `setInterval` (20 s, anchored on `globalThis` like the database handle and the terminal registry — a `bun --hot` reload must not leave a second interval running). Each tick claims every task due at `now` and dispatches it. The Calendar button in both sidebars used to open a mock dialog: a textarea with no state, a "Schedule Task" button that only closed the window, and decorative kind/interval controls.
- **A fire is a normal prompt through the normal spawn path.** `runScheduledTask` opens a child with `startNewRpcSession` (fresh conversation) or `startRpcSession` when the task names a `sessionId`, sends the task's model snapshot (`set_model` / `set_thinking_level`), then `prompt` — exactly what a queued follow-up delivery sends. Nothing bypasses the registry, so the approval mode, the idle reaper and the stream-status row all behave as they do for a user-initiated prompt. Verified end to end on a real server: a `once` task and an `every 1m` task each produced a real omp session whose transcript reads `user: <prompt>` / `ai: <answer>`.
- **A claim is a WRITE, and it advances the schedule in the same transaction** (`claimDueTasks`). That is what makes two ticks — or two Kabmo instances on the shared database — safe: the second sees the row already moved past its own `now` and takes nothing. A `once` task is disabled by its own claim (`next_run_at = NULL, enabled = 0`) rather than by the dispatcher, so a one-shot whose dispatch failed cannot resurrect on the next tick or on a restart.
- **`next_run_at` is stored, not derived.** A `once` task's fire time IS its spec, a paused task has none, and a spent one-shot is a third state — none of which a re-parse could distinguish. The task list renders them differently on purpose: `Paused` versus `Ran once — no next run` versus a real `Next <time>`.
- **Editing a schedule or resuming a task RECOMPUTES the clock from now** (`updateScheduledTask`). Keeping the pending value would fire once on the cadence the user just replaced (a `1h` → `1d` edit), and re-enabling a paused interval task would fire immediately for every interval it spent paused.
- **The run record is the only place a failure exists.** A task is claimed before it is dispatched, so a spawn that throws can never be retried by a later tick; `finishScheduledRun` writes `error` + the message and the modal renders it under the row. A manual "Run now" is recorded with `countRun: false`, so testing a task does not inflate its fire count, and it deliberately does NOT move `next_run_at` — the user is asking "does this work", not asking to skip the scheduled fire.
- **One grammar, shared by the form and the runtime** (`src/shared/lib/schedule/parse.ts`). `every` takes `30s`/`5m`/`1h30m`/`2 days`; `cron` takes 5 fields with `*`, `N`, `a-b`, `a,b,c`, star-slash-n and `a-b/n`. Two rules are load-bearing and unit-tested: **when BOTH day-of-month and day-of-week are restricted the day matches if EITHER does** (Vixie cron — `0 0 13 * 5` fires on the 13th AND every Friday, not only Friday the 13th), and `nextCronOccurrence` walks DAYS with whole-month skips (a `0 0 29 2 *` is 1461 day-steps away; a minute walk would be 2.1 M iterations and an impossible date such as `0 0 30 2 *` reports "never" rather than hanging). The next fire is strictly AFTER the cursor, so a task that fires at exactly its own `lastRunAt` advances instead of re-firing forever. The 1-minute floor is enforced because the tick is 20 s.
- **The modal validates with the same parser it previews with** (`TaskForm`), so the form cannot accept a spec the route would refuse — and the route validates again (`createScheduledTask`/`updateScheduledTask`), because a stale client is always possible. Verified in the browser: `30s` renders "The shortest interval is 1 minute.", a red border and a disabled submit; a valid `2h` renders "Next run in 2h · <local time>".
- **The model snapshot is resolved SERVER-side when the request carries none** (`resolveDefaultTaskModel`): the composer's persisted pick plus the persisted access mode, or `null` when nothing is stored (omp's own default then applies). A scheduled run happens with nobody watching, so the snapshot has to exist, and the value the user last chose in the composer is the only honest default. An edit never rewrites it.
- **The cwd is resolved at WRITE time and stored on the row** (`resolveTaskCwd` → `project_path` else the folder name). A folder deleted after the task was created must still let it fire in the directory it was created for; storing only the folder id would leave the runtime falling back to the server's own cwd, which is how a scheduled job silently runs in the wrong project. No folder chosen means the server's cwd, and the form says so ("Server default directory").
- **State lives in `scheduled_tasks` / `scheduled_task_runs`** (`src/server/lib/schedule/schema.server.ts`, called from `initSchema` beside the BTW tables). Runs are capped at `RUN_HISTORY_LIMIT` (50) per task, pruned after each close, or an interval task would grow the table without bound. API: `GET/POST/PATCH/DELETE /api/schedule` (`?id=` for the single-task verbs, `?id=…&run=now` to fire immediately) and `GET /api/schedule/:taskId/runs`.
- **The toolbar badge is a derived read, and it follows the write.** `useScheduledTaskCount` reads the schedule realtime topic — the same one the modal reads, so the badge cannot disagree with the list and a mutation reaches it with no event and no poll. Verified live in the browser with no reload: `4 armed` → pause a row → `3 armed`, and the count survived a full server restart (the row is the state).
- **The runtime's dependencies are INJECTED** (`ScheduleDeps`), never mocked through `mock.module` in tests. `mock.module` swaps a registry entry for the whole `bun test` run: mocking the RPC manager in `runtime.server.test.ts` broke six unrelated suites with "Export named … not found" (measured), which is why `runScheduledTask(task, { deps })` takes them and `runtime.server.test.ts` drives the real dispatch logic with a fake session handle.

### Waiting for Another Session (composer, new chats only)
- **A held prompt is a SERVER row, not a client timer.** `src/server/lib/waiting/` owns it (`waiting_prompts`, created by `ensureWaitingSchema` from `initSchema`) for the reason the scheduled tasks are server-owned: a prompt that waits must survive a reload, a closed tab and a second Kabmo instance. `runtime.server.ts` is the clock — a 5 s tick plus two event triggers — and `store.server.ts`'s `claimWaitingPrompt` is the whole concurrency answer: the UPDATE that flips `waiting → dispatched` reports whether it won, so two sweeps (or two instances on the shared database) can never spawn two sessions for one prompt.
- **"Finished" is five conditions, and `finish` alone is not one of them** (`waiting/settle.ts`). The stream row must not be `stream`; a live wrapper must not be `isBusy()` (a compaction, a subagent or an open ask dialog keeps it busy with no row to say so); the dependency's follow-up queue must be empty; no delivery slot may be armed for it (the run end arms one 500 ms out); and its last status write must be older than `SETTLE_GRACE_MS` (1.5 s), because `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` has one-second resolution. That clock is read by `waiting/row-clock.server.ts`, NOT carried on `SessionStreamState` — that payload is the `sidebar:status` wire contract and its exact shape is pinned by `stream-state.server.test.ts`, so a field added for one caller would ride every frame for nobody. Each condition is a measured way a prompt would otherwise be delivered into a running turn — where omp refuses it and the message is lost. A row with NO stream entry counts as finished: nothing can be waited on, and holding the prompt forever would be worse.
- **The dependency is a session ID, re-read at every sweep** — never a snapshot of its state. A session that looks finished picks up a queued follow-up a moment later, and the two triggers exist for exactly that: a terminal `agent_end` (`requestWaitingSweep` in `terminal-settle.ts`) and the queue's own drain (`delivery.server.ts`). Both go through `waiting/sweep-trigger.ts`, a `globalThis` slot, because the runtime already imports the queue and a direct edge back would close a cycle.
- **The session's `send` is passed as a CLOSURE, never as a detached method.** The wrapper reaches its runtime state through `this`; `applyModelSnapshot(session.send, …)` ran it against `undefined` and every prompt failed with `undefined is not an object (evaluating 'host.restarting')`. Pinned by a test whose fake session is an object METHOD and asserts the receiver survives.
- **A failed dispatch is retried, then REPORTED** (`MAX_DISPATCH_ATTEMPTS` 3). The prompt is returned to `waiting` while attempts remain — nothing was delivered, so it is still wanted — and `failWaitingPrompt` accepts a row that was already claimed, because a spawn that succeeded and a delivery that then threw has still failed. Deleting the dependency fails every prompt waiting on it by name (`failWaitingOn` in `delete.server.ts`), rather than leaving a row waiting for a session that no longer exists.
- **The prompt is assembled by `queue/payload.ts`, shared with the follow-up queue.** Both send a prompt nobody typed in this tab, from the same persisted shape (an attachment's bytes live in `dataBase64`, its text in `content` — the live `File` never survives the row). A second copy is how one path inlines a text file and the other sends a bare filename.
- **The picker lists only sessions with a run in flight, and the STATUS MAP is that list** (`WaitingDependencyPicker`, beside the workspace picker in `EmptyWorkspacePrompt` — both answer "what is this not-yet-started chat about"). The folders only supply names: a freshly spawned session has its `stream` row the moment the prompt is dispatched, while omp's transcript — what the sidebar structure is scanned from — lands seconds later. Driving the list off `folder.sessions` therefore offered nothing in exactly the case the feature exists for, measured where the running session was a PENDING row and its folder listed no sessions at all. A wait on an idle session would be released by the next sweep, so offering it would promise a wait that cannot happen.
