Measured on the failing CI leg: the placeholder was never drawn and the assertion timed out for 15s while the panel was already correct, and the spec then passed on retry. The session-state GET is the gate (the blob is what the click needs); reopening a file that already has a tab reuses that tab, so the placeholder adds nothing.
- **A "platform limit" is a claim to verify, not a reason to skip.** Both `terminal-capacity.e2e.ts` specs and `terminal.e2e.ts` skipped Windows on the belief that the PTY child could not stay alive there. It could: the cause was Kabmo's own spawn (`detached` on Windows = `DETACHED_PROCESS` = no console, so `cmd.exe` exited 1 immediately), and once fixed all three specs pass on that leg — the ConPTY byte-difference the `terminal.e2e.ts` skip also cited never mattered, because neither of its assertions compares bytes (a prompt character class and a unique marker). The cap's WINDOWS BRANCH stays covered independently by `store-liveness.test.ts`, which injects `platform: 'win32'`. A skip that outlives its cause is worse than no skip: it reports the platform as unverifiable and hides the product bug that made it look that way. Before adding one, reproduce the failure against the real spawn and name the exact option/API that causes it.
- **Assert the observable that actually changes, not the control you clicked.** The editor's saved state is an icon swap and its `aria-label` does not move, so the spec asserts the FILE ON DISK; xterm paints to a WebGL canvas with no text nodes, so the terminal spec asserts the terminal socket's frames. An assertion on the button, or on `.xterm-rows`, would have been vacuous.
- **A dialog's state is initialized by MOUNTING it fresh, never by a reset effect — and this spec is what found that.** `NewWorkspaceModal` kept one mounted form and cleared its fields in a `useEffect` keyed on `isOpen`; Preact runs effects AFTER paint, so the dialog was on screen and its fields focusable before the reset landed, and anything written in that window was discarded. Measured through this layer: a path typed into the field was wiped and the listing stayed on the home directory; the same bug in `DirectoryList`'s new-folder field left it empty with the submit button disabled. The fix is structural — `if (!isOpen) return null` and mount the form — so there is no ordering question to get wrong. The related lesson is that `fill()` is a legitimate way to find this: it writes synchronously after the element exists, which is exactly the window an effect-based reset loses.
- **`getByRole(name:)` matches a SUBSTRING, so a fixture's locator handler can click the wrong thing.** `installOverlayHandler` addresses the update popup's "Later" button; a spec that put a row named `appeared-later` on screen had that row matched and CLICKED by the handler, which navigated the dialog into the folder and made the row vanish — a failure that looked like a broken refresh. The handler's locator carries `exact: true`. Check every locator used in `addLocatorHandler` (and any handler that fires on a page-wide match) for this: the popup's own control is exactly "Later".
- **Two runners, disjoint globs, on purpose.** `bun test` owns `*.spec.ts`; Playwright owns `*.e2e.ts` (`testMatch` in `tests/e2e/playwright.config.ts`). The first version of this layer used `.spec.ts` and `bun test` imported the Playwright file into its own process, where `test.describe` throws "Playwright Test did not expect test.describe() to be called here". Never let the globs overlap — neither runner can be told to ignore a file the other owns without also hiding a real suite.
- **The runner is Playwright's own, in `tests/e2e/`, never `bun test`.** A browser spec needs a per-file context, a per-file server and a trace artifact — a lifecycle Bun's runner does not own, and one that fights its single-process, shared-`globalThis` model (the reason `test-support/isolated-db.ts` exists).
- **`fixtures/server.ts` boots a REAL server subprocess**, not an in-process Elysia app, because the thing under test is the assembled product. It is runtime-agnostic (`node:child_process`, `node:net`, `globalThis.fetch`) — it runs in a Playwright WORKER, which is Node, so a `Bun.spawn` there is a ReferenceError.
- **Isolation is the contract, and it is why `getDatabasePath` was fixed.** Every path the server writes is redirected under one temp root removed at teardown: `KABMO_DATA_DIR`, `KABMO_DB_PATH`, `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR`, plus `SYNC_WORKSPACE=false`. `getDatabasePath` put the MOCK branch BEFORE the `KABMO_DB_PATH` check, so a `MOCK=true` server started from a checkout wrote the repository's own `workspace.db` no matter what the caller set — verified, and pinned by `src/server/db.server.test.ts`. An explicit override now wins over BOTH defaults. Do not reorder those branches; a test that leaks into `~/.kabmo` or the checkout's `workspace.db` corrupts the developer's machine.
- **Readiness is polled on `GET /api/health`, and the PORT is compared**, never parsed from the boot log. The route is a stable shape (`ok`, `mock`, `port`); a log line is prose a future edit may reword. Comparing the port is what stops a stale instance or a lost port race from being adopted silently.
- **`fixtures/fake-omp.ts` is the keystone, and it is a real protocol CLIENT, not a mock.** It speaks omp's NDJSON wire (`ready`, `{type:'response'}` with `command`/`success`/`data`, and the session event frames), so the code under test is the real spawn path, frame reader, frame fold, realtime topic and renderer. A double that stubbed any of those would test the double — the same argument `test-support/realtime-server.ts` makes. It honors the argv Kabmo actually builds (`--mode rpc-ui --cwd … --resume … --approval-mode …`) and prompts select behaviour: `delay:<ms>`, `tool:<name>`, `edit:<pairs>`, `ask:<text>`, `error:<text>`, `retry:<n>`, `slow`, default `Response to: <prompt>`. **`edit:<pairs>`** emits a real `edit` card whose `details.diff` is omp's EXCERPT dialect of that many removed/added PAIRS — every row a change, because the card renders `contentMode: 'diff'` and a patch of context lines collapses back under the 288px ceiling, where the scroller's range is 0 and a spec built on it proves nothing (measured). It is the only way a browser spec can observe the diff card's own scroller.
- **The fake must emit the FULL accumulated `message_update` frames Kabmo requests.** `configureChild` sends `set_event_filter {messageUpdates:"full"}`, so every `message_update` carries the whole message again — that is what the client folds. The fake re-sends the accumulated content on each delta (and the tool-call fixture re-sends its block with the name/id), so a spec observes the same shapes a real child sends.
- **`FAKE_OMP_RECORD_PATH` appends every RECEIVED frame as JSONL**, so a spec can assert what Kabmo sent (that the event filter was requested, that a reload reached a live child) rather than inferring it.
- **Two modes, chosen per spec.** `mock` uses the seeded demo presets and needs no `omp` install (fast, no fake). `real` runs `MOCK=false` with `KABMO_OMP_BIN` pointed at a shim that execs `fake-omp.ts` under Bun — the shim exists because the server spawns the path directly, so it must be executable. There is no model and no network in either mode.
- **Fixture data is created through the REAL API** (`fixtures/seed.ts` → `POST /api/folders`), never by writing the database. A hand-written row skips the route that resolves `project_path` and the scan the sidebar reads, so it tests a state the app cannot produce.
- **Every temp directory is REGISTERED the moment it exists, because the per-spec teardown is not the whole story.** `fixtures/temp-root.ts` owns the registry and is the only place this layer mints a temp dir (`makeTempDir` for test scope, `makeProcessTempDir` for the compiled fake `omp`, which outlives the test that built it). Three shapes leak without it, and each was measured: a spec that FAILS or is retried mid-body skips every `dispose()` at the end of that body (a probe spec seeding a workspace and a working repo left 2 + 2 directories on each of its two attempts), a Ctrl-C kills the worker before any teardown runs, and a SIGKILLed worker runs nothing at all. So the registry has two scopes and one sweep: the `_tempRoots` auto fixture opens a test scope before every body and sweeps it after — which is the failure path a `dispose()` cannot reach — and process exit plus SIGINT/SIGTERM sweep the process scope. `auto: true` is what makes the ORDER right (an automatic fixture tears down after the spec's own), so `server.stop()` has already released the directory handles; a workspace the server still had open would fail to delete on Windows. `HarnessServer.stop()` still removes its own run root and then calls `untrackTempDir`, and its removals are individually try/caught — a fixture teardown that throws is reported as the SPEC's failure. **The one case no in-worker hook can reach is a SIGKILL, and that is `globalSetup`'s job**: `sweepStaleTempDirs()` runs once before the first worker and deletes `omc-e2e-*` directories older than two hours (mtime, not birthtime — a directory keeps being written while its spec runs), which is what stops the accumulation. Measured on this machine before the fix: 964 directories and 1.3 GB in `$TMPDIR` over four days, dominated by 310 seeded workspaces and 369 run roots; after it, a passing run, a failing run and a Ctrl-C'd run each leave 0. `sweepStaleTempDirs` takes an injectable `root` because it deletes BY NAME — the first version of its unit test ran against the real `$TMPDIR` and reclaimed the developer's 964 directories, which is exactly the hazard the option removes. `fixtures/temp-root.test.ts` covers both scopes and every sweep rule against a private root, and `tests/e2e/tsconfig.json` excludes `**/*.test.ts` because those files import `bun:test` while the program deliberately has `types: ["node"]` only (adding `bun-types` there would also let a SPEC reach `Bun.*`, a ReferenceError in a Playwright worker).
- **`data-testid` only where no stable accessible name exists.** The preference is `getByRole` + accessible name (the toolbars already carry `title`/`aria-label` on every icon-only button), then visible text, then a testid for a container to scope a query, a virtualized row, or a canvas. Measured: `src/` had ZERO testids before this layer; the set added is `app-root` (both layouts) and `composer-input`. Message rows already carry `id={msg.id}`, so a spec addresses a row by its id rather than by a new attribute. Add a testid as part of the spec that needs it, with a stable domain name — never speculatively, never layout detail.
- **The E2E project is typechecked separately** (`bun run typecheck:e2e` → `tsc -p tests/e2e`) because it runs under Node, not Bun, and the root tsconfig covers only `src/` and `packages/`. `ci.yml` runs this gate so a broken spec fails the cheap job, not only the browser one.
- **CI runs it on every push to `main`** (`.github/workflows/e2e.yml`), on a pull request only when the `e2e` label is applied, plus nightly and manual dispatch. A browser job is minutes, not seconds, so the label is the cheap-PR escape hatch — but a push to `main` is a merge, and it is verified there like the other gates. The release job's own `[skip ci]` commits do not re-trigger it (verified). Chromium is required; firefox/webkit are opt-in projects not yet run in CI. Failure artifacts (trace, screenshot, HTML report) are uploaded only on failure.

### Route Method Contract
- Route modules keep the ported Remix shape: an exported `loader` (GET) and/or `action` (mutating verbs). **`action` owns method dispatch** — it branches on `request.method` and returns its own `405 { error: 'Method not allowed' }`.
- Because of that, `bindingsFor`/`actionBindings` mount `action` on **all four** mutating verbs, and add a 405 GET fallback when the path has no `loader`. Registering only the "supported" verb would turn a wrong-verb call into a 404 and hide the real problem.
- **One parameter name per path position.** Elysia's router rejects `/api/files/:sessionId` and `/api/files/:fileId/toggle` in the same tree. When two routes share a segment position, unify the name (the handlers map it to their own local variable). Current shared names: `sessions/:sessionId` (also carries a folder id for `GET /api/sessions/:sessionId`), `files/:fileId`.
- **A STATIC segment that shares a position with a parameter route is registered BEFORE it, and as its own explicit binding.** `/api/sessions/batch-delete` and `/api/folders/batch-delete` are POST-only entries placed above the `:sessionId` / `:folderId` families, because a parameter route winning the match answers 405 (its handler dispatches on verb) and the batch silently becomes "method not allowed" instead of an unknown path. `actionBindings` is deliberately not used for them either — it mounts all four mutating verbs, and a batch verb should not answer PUT/PATCH.

---

## Agent Operational Workflows

### Diagnostic Protocol on Build Failures
1. **Log Scrape**: Scrape build output from bun runner (matching `exit 1` conditions).
2. **Error Isolation**: Target the root failure point (e.g., missing package imports, type mismatch, or invalid asset clipping).
3. **Patch Generation**: Formulate a runnable bun terminal command (e.g., `bun add @superdesign/svg-geometry@latest && bun run build`).
4. **Execution in Kabmo**: The user or agent triggers execution directly in the right sidebar panel.

### Code Style & Persistence Guidelines
- Use the CSS variable system defined in `src/client/tailwind.css` (`var(--theme-ink)`, `var(--theme-paper)`, etc.) and standard Tailwind classes mapped to them (`bg-paper`, `text-ink`, `border-ink/20`).
- The application supports multiple themes (e.g., E-Ink Paper Monochrome, One Dark Pro Soft). **DO NOT** hardcode raw hex colors like `#141310` or `#faf8f3` in component files.
- Semantic states are expressed purely through these theme variables.
- Every theme write goes through `applyDocumentTheme` (`src/client/hooks/ui/theme.ts`): it sets `<html data-theme>` and dispatches `omp:theme-changed`. Writing `document.documentElement.dataset.theme` directly leaves runtime consumers that cannot see CSS (the mermaid hydrator repaints diagrams per theme) on the previous palette.
- The only allowable chroma (outside of dark theme) is the signal red variable `var(--theme-error)` (`text-error`, `bg-error`) reserved for failures and error messages. Syntax highlighting is an explicit, user-approved exception: it is provided by Shiki with the dual-theme pair `one-light` (light themes) + `one-dark-pro` (dark themes). Token colors arrive as `--shiki-light` / `--shiki-dark` CSS variables on `.shiki` spans — consume them in `src/client/tailwind.css` under `[data-theme]`, never hardcode token hex, and never reintroduce `--syntax-*` variables or `.token.*` classes.

### Changelog Policy (Release-Only)
- **NEVER edit `CHANGELOG.md` during normal work** — not for a feature, a fix, a refactor, a typo, or a version-less "unreleased" note. A task ends at code plus the gates in rule 8; the changelog is not part of it.
- `CHANGELOG.md` is written **only when cutting a release**, and releases are cut by `.github/workflows/release.yml` on every push to `main` — so since `0.6.0` the file is written **by the pipeline, never by hand**: `release.config.mjs` derives each `## [x.y.z] — YYYY-MM-DD` section from the Conventional Commits since the last tag, above the previous release (newest first), carrying the `[Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/)` categories that apply (`### Added`, `### Changed`, `### Fixed`, `### Removed`). The section order, the header and the leading `### BREAKING CHANGES` group are that config's `commitTypes` and `writerOpts`; a new category means editing both.
- New sections link their compare range **inline** (`## [0.6.0](…/compare/v0.5.0...v0.6.0) — …`) and their bullets are commit subjects, not the hand-written prose the sections before `0.6.0` use, so the `[x.y.z]:` reference block at the file's tail stops at the last hand-written release. Never add to, reword or reformat a section that already shipped — it describes what shipped, not what a later change adjusted.
- Write entries as released behavior: a bullet **is** the commit subject, so the subject carries the changelog — name the behavior that changed, never a restatement of the diff. A type marked `hidden` in `release.config.mjs` (`chore`, `style`, `test`, `build`, `ci`) appears nowhere, and a type carrying `release: false` in `release/release-rules.js` cannot cut a version on its own — not even with a `!` on it, because the rules there shadow the analyzer's own `{ breaking: true }` fallback for every type this repository uses.
- A bullet credits its author — `… ([c148bc5](…)) (thanks [@octocat](https://github.com/octocat))` — unless that author is the repository owner, whose own commits carry the bulk of every release. `release/contributors.js` resolves the handle offline from GitHub privacy addresses (`NNN+login@users.noreply.github.com`) and asks the API about every other author, which is why the release job's `GITHUB_TOKEN` is what credits a contributor who commits with a real mailbox; bots (`[bot]` logins, `type: 'Bot'`) and unverified addresses get nothing, and a failed lookup never fails the release.

### Conventional Commit Type Selection
Every commit uses Conventional Commits — pick the type from what the diff actually does to observable behavior, not from how big it is:
- **`feat`** — new user-visible behavior: a feature, endpoint, setting option, or capability that did not exist before. A change that only reorganizes internals while the UI/API output stays identical is NOT a feat.
- **`fix`** — corrects existing behavior that was wrong: a bug, regression, wrong result, crash, or broken edge case. There must be a demonstrable "before was wrong" state; a behavior change that is merely a decision is not a fix.
- **`refactor`** — restructures code with zero external behavior change: renames, file moves/extractions, deduplication, type tightening, performance work with no visible effect.
- **`perf`** — the primary intent is a measurable performance improvement, with behavior unchanged.
- **`style`** — formatting/whitespace/import order only; no logic change. (`hidden` in `release.config.mjs`.)
- **`test`** — adds or repairs tests only; no production code change. (`hidden`.)
- **`build`** — toolchain, dependencies, build scripts (`package.json`, `bunfig.toml`, `scripts/build-client.ts`, `bun.lock`). (`hidden`.)
- **`ci`** — pipeline config only (`.github/workflows/`, `release.config.mjs`). (`hidden`.)
- **`chore`** — maintenance that fits none of the above (ignore files, secondary scripts). (`hidden`.)
- **`docs`** — documentation only (README, AGENTS.md, comments).
- **`BREAKING CHANGE`** — append `!` and a footer when a change requires callers/users to act: removed/renamed API, route, setting, or a changed contract. The changelog's `### BREAKING CHANGES` group comes from this footer. Two limits are enforced by `release/release-rules.js`, not by convention: **`!` releases a major version only on `feat`, `feature`, `fix`, `perf` and `refactor`** — on `chore`, `docs`, `ci` and the rest it releases nothing at all, so a breaking change to the build or the pipeline belongs in the type that carries it — and **the footer is read case-sensitively**, so it is written `BREAKING CHANGE: …` (or `BREAKING-CHANGE:`) at the start of a line. A lowercase `breaking-change` mention in prose is not a footer; before the pattern was pinned, one such line in a `docs` body cut a major release by itself.
| Mixed change in one commit: split into separate commits when the files are separable; if not, use the dominant type and note the rest in the body. The subject names the behavior that changed (it becomes a changelog bullet verbatim), the body explains the why/how — never restate the diff. `release/bump.test.js` asserts the whole table, and `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs it (with the other three gates) on every pull request and on `main`.

### Package Releases (kabmo-sdk / kabmo-ui)

- **The npm names carry the `kabmo-` prefix because the org does.** The scope is `@kabmo-labs` and every package in it is a Kabmo package, so `@kabmo-labs/kabmo-sdk` / `@kabmo-labs/kabmo-ui` state which product they belong to instead of leaving a bare `plugin-sdk`/`ui` to be read as the org's generic library. The directories follow the names (`packages/kabmo-sdk`, `packages/kabmo-ui`), and so do the release tag prefixes (`kabmo-sdk/v1.2.3`) — a tag is the package's identity in the workflow, and a tag that disagrees with the manifest name publishes a version under a name nobody wrote down.
- **Every manifest starts at `0.0.1`, and a seed tag is what keeps the line there.** The app, both packages and all three bundled plugins declare `0.0.1`. semantic-release has **no option for a first version**: `get-next-version.js` returns the hardcoded `FIRST_RELEASE` (`1.0.0`) whenever the branch has no tag matching `tagFormat`. Verified against a throwaway repository: no tag → `1.0.0`; a `v0.0.1` tag present → the next `feat` cuts `0.1.0` and stays on the 0.x line. So the 0.0.1 lineage begins with a `v0.0.1` tag (and `kabmo-sdk/v0.0.1`, `kabmo-ui/v0.0.1` for the packages) on the release commit; without it the first pipeline run publishes `1.0.0` while `package.json` says `0.0.1`, and `publish.yml` refuses that mismatch. The retired local tags `v1.0.0` / `plugin-sdk/v1.0.0` / `ui/v1.0.0` point at a commit that is not an ancestor of `HEAD` and were never pushed — they match no `tagFormat` in use, so they neither seed nor block anything.
- **The pre-rename specifiers still resolve, and they must.** `@kabmo-labs/plugin-sdk` and `@kabmo-labs/ui` are already baked into every plugin bundle on disk, so `RUNTIME_SLOT_BY_SPECIFIER` (`panels/shim.ts`), `kabmoPackageDirs` (`panels/link.server.ts`) and `runningVersions` (`panels/compat.server.ts`) all name them beside the new ones. Removing them would fail an installed plugin at load with `Cannot load … must be loaded by Kabmo`, which is a rename breaking the thing the rename was for.

- **The published packages are versioned from commits too — never bump them by hand.** They are released by the SAME workflow as the app, `.github/workflows/release.yml`, whose `packages` job runs `node release/release-packages.mjs` and cuts a release per package through semantic-release. The version rules, the changelog shape and the contributor credit are the app's, reused rather than restated: `release/packages.mjs` reads `commitTypes`, `parserOpts` and `releaseRules` out of `release.config.mjs`.
- **The app releases AFTER the packages, in one chained run, and the order is load-bearing.** The app does not ship `packages/` (see its `files` list) — at runtime it resolves `@kabmo-labs/*` from its own `node_modules`, which is the version `bun.lock` records for those workspace packages, and a `workspace:` spec is substituted from the LOCK rather than the manifest. Releasing the app first therefore publishes stale pins: measured, `kabmo@4.1.1` on npm declares both packages at `1.0.0` while they are at `2.0.0` (and `@kabmo-labs/kabmo-ui@2.0.0` declared the SDK at `1.0.0` while the SDK was `2.0.0`). The `packages` job commits the refreshed `bun.lock`, and the `app` job `needs: packages` and fast-forwards to that commit before it installs and releases. The workflow is four jobs: `packages` → `packages-publish`, and `packages` → `app` → `app-publish`; a failed package release stops the app's, because the pin state would be unknown.
- **One workflow means one concurrency group, and that is the point.** A concurrency group is not a lock: it is a queue that DROPS pending members as it grows. An earlier revision split the releases across two workflows and shared one group between them so they could not race on their release commits; measured, two pushes in quick succession put four runs in the one group and GitHub cancelled the app's runs to keep the queue bounded, so the app stopped releasing entirely. With both releases in one workflow there is no second group to collide with, and each job's `git fetch origin main && git merge --ff-only FETCH_HEAD` covers the intra-run ordering.
- **Both release jobs fast-forward their checkout before installing, and that is not optional.** semantic-release refuses to release when the local branch is behind the remote — "The local branch main is behind the remote one, therefore a new version won't be published." — and it exits 0, so the run reports success while releasing nothing. Measured on this pipeline's first real run: the app's release had landed first and the packages run silently cut no release.
- **A release cuts the tag and the GitHub Release; the publish is dispatched.** The release commits are pushed with `GITHUB_TOKEN`, which raises no workflow run (only `workflow_dispatch`/`repository_dispatch` are exempt), so the `packages-publish` job calls `gh workflow run publish-plugins.yml -f packages=…` for exactly the packages that released, and `app-publish` calls `gh workflow run publish.yml -f tag=…`. Both publish workflows stay separate and keep the `npm` environment's approval gate, the gates and the tarball check.
- **The two release jobs repeat their setup steps rather than sharing them through YAML anchors.** GitHub does support anchors, but an alias carrying an entire step into a DIFFERENT job is not a shape the documented examples cover, and a parser that rejects it means no release at all, silently, on the push that needed one. Six short steps duplicated is the cheaper failure mode.
- **A rehearsal in a scratch clone still pushes to PRODUCTION.** `@semantic-release/git` pushes to `repositoryUrl`, which semantic-release reads from `package.json`'s `repository` field — **not** from the clone's `origin`. Measured: a "scratch" run with a local bare origin pushed two release commits and two tags to `github.com/kabmo-labs/kabmo`, and the branch had to be reverted by hand (nothing was published — the tag push raises no workflow run, and this pipeline never publishes). `assertReleaseEnvironment` in `release/packages.js` now refuses to run outside CI unless `KABMO_RELEASE_ALLOW_NON_CI=1`; **rehearse with `--dry-run`**, which skips every `prepare` step and pushes nothing.
- **`@semantic-release/npm` cannot write the version here, and that was found by rehearsing.** It shells out to `npm version`, and npm does not support the `workspace:` protocol this workspace declares its SDK dependency with: the run dies with `EUNSUPPORTEDPROTOCOL Unsupported URL Type "workspace:"` on a *sibling* package's manifest, after npm reifies the tree (it removed `node_modules` outright in the first attempt). `release/version-package.mjs` is a local plugin that writes the one field instead, with no package manager in the loop — which is safe precisely because this pipeline does not publish. `bun publish` in `publish-plugins.yml` is what packs the protocol correctly (verified: the published 1.0.0 tarball carries the exact SDK version).
- **Three mechanisms narrow one repository's history to one package, and all three are needed.** (1) A per-package `tagFormat` (`kabmo-sdk/v${version}`) makes the range start at that package's own last tag. (2) A **path filter** in front of the analyzer and the notes generator keeps only commits that touched the package directory — a tag range still contains every app commit made in between, and a `feat(panels)` there would bump the package's minor version while its own code did not change (measured on this repo: 12 commits in `kabmo-sdk/v1.0.0..HEAD`, 2 of them touching the SDK). (3) The local version plugin writes the new version into the package's own `package.json`, and its `verifyConditions` refuses a `private` package rather than cutting a release with nothing to publish.
- **The filter is applied by WRAPPING the two plugins, not by a shareable config.** `context.commits` is what both read and semantic-release hands the same context to every plugin in a step, so the only place a subset can be introduced is inside the plugin call. The wrappers are **step keys** (`analyzeCommits`/`generateNotes`), never entries in `plugins`: `validatePlugin` requires a `plugins` array's first element to be a string or plain object, while `validateStep` accepts a function — a function in `plugins` fails with `EPLUGINSCONF`, and the step keys are also the only place a function can carry options.
- **`release/commits.js` is the path lookup, because nothing upstream has it.** semantic-release's own collector (`git-log-parser`) reads a fixed field set — hash, author, committer, subject, body — and **no paths**. Two whole-range calls: `git log` once for messages and identities, and `git diff-tree --stdin --name-only -r --root` once for every hash (one process, and measured identical paths to `git log --name-only`). `--root` is what makes the repository's first commit list its paths at all. The scratch-repo test in `release/packages.test.js` is what pins it.
- **`scopeCommits` takes the repository as an argument** (`context.cwd`), not a path derived from the module. They are the same in the pipeline, but a caller whose commits came from elsewhere would otherwise have its hashes looked up in the wrong tree, where every commit reads as path-less — and a path-less commit is deliberately **kept** (a merge or an empty commit carries no change of its own, and dropping it would drop a `revert` git recorded that way). That "keep everything" failure is silent; the test caught it.
- **Catalog order is load-bearing and a failure STOPS the run.** `@kabmo-labs/kabmo-ui` declares `"@kabmo-labs/kabmo-sdk": "workspace:*"`, and `release/packages.js` is the one place a package's directory, tag prefix and manifest name are stated (`release/packages.test.js` asserts them against the tree). `.github/scripts/publish-plugin-packages.sh` exits on the first failure rather than publishing a `kabmo-ui` after a failed SDK release.
- **A `workspace:` spec is substituted from `bun.lock`, NOT from the manifest — so bumping a version without refreshing the lock publishes a stale dependency.** `bun.lock` records a version for every workspace package, and `bun publish` reads that record. Measured in production: the version plugin bumped `packages/kabmo-sdk` to 2.0.0 and committed it, but the lock still said 1.0.0, so the next release published `@kabmo-labs/kabmo-ui@2.0.0` declaring `"@kabmo-labs/kabmo-sdk": "1.0.0"` while the SDK was 2.0.0 on the registry — an install resolving the old SDK, which is exactly the incompatibility the 2.0.0 release existed to announce. Reproduced in isolation: bump `packages/a/package.json` without `bun install` and `bun pm pack` in a dependent `packages/b` still writes the old version. `release/version-package.mjs` therefore runs `bun install --lockfile-only` after each version write (`--lockfile-only` because the job has already installed and only the lock's record needs to change), and `@semantic-release/git` commits `bun.lock` beside the manifest — without the commit the refresh would be discarded and the next release would re-publish the stale pin. `release/packages.test.js` builds a two-package fixture and asserts the dependent package packs the NEW version.
- **A manual release still works and is still the escape hatch**: bump the version in the package's `package.json`, push `kabmo-sdk/vX.Y.Z` (or `kabmo-ui/vX.Y.Z`), and `publish-plugins.yml` publishes it. That workflow also takes `packages` (what the release workflow dispatches), `tag` (re-run one release whose publish failed, without re-creating it) and `package` (a hand-run fallback) — resolved in that order, and the tag/version match is checked for the `tag` input as well as for a tag push.

- **The bundled marketplace plugins are released by a version BUMP, not a publish — and the `marketplace` job in `release.yml` does it.** The job is named `marketplace` rather than `plugins` on purpose: `publish-plugins.yml` already means the npm packages `@kabmo-labs/kabmo-sdk` and `@kabmo-labs/kabmo-ui`, and a job called `plugins` beside a workflow called "Publish Plugin Packages" reads as the same thing. Nothing here is published. All three (`marketplace/plugins/{command-runner,session-info,stats}`) are `private: true` and reach a user inside the app's own tarball (`getBundledMarketplaceDir()` is `<package>/marketplace`; `bun pm pack` carries `marketplace/plugins/*`). There is no registry for them, so the version their manifest declares is the **entire delivery mechanism**: `syncBundledPlugins` compares the installed manifest's version against the store's and applies the new copy only when the store's is strictly NEWER (`isNewer`). A fix whose version did not move is therefore skipped by every existing install, forever — measured in this repository before the job existed: the `stats` sticky-header fix (`09e097f`) and the `session-info` icon fix (`283802b`) both shipped with their versions untouched, and `git log -L` shows their watermarks still at the original 1.0.0.
- **The job sits BETWEEN `packages` and the shell bump, and that position is the design.** The chain is `packages → marketplace → android → apk-build → app`; the app job fast-forwards to the commits these jobs make (`Sync to the current remote`) and only then runs `bun pm pack`, so the bumped manifest is inside the tarball a user installs. A bump landing after the app packed would reach existing installs one release late. `tests/ci-gates.test.ts` pins every hop, because the transitive gate check cannot see ordering.

- **The Android shell is released the same way, and for the same reason — `release/android.js` + `release/release-android.mjs`, run by the `android` job.** `packages/android/version.properties` is the ONE place the shell's `versionName` is declared: the build reads it at configuration time, the shell appends it to its WebView User-Agent as `KabmoAndroid/<versionName>`, and `publish-android.yml` names the asset `Kabmo-<versionName>-android.apk` from it — so the version the console compares (`versionFromAssetName` against `shellVersion`) is the same number by construction. **It used to be the app's release TAG**, which tied two independent cadences together: a shell-only fix could not reach an installed app unless the server also cut a version, and a server release shipped an APK whose version said nothing about whether the shell had changed. The bump level comes from the same analyzer, the write is surgical on the one `versionName=` line, and a `chore`/`docs`/`test`-only change asks for none. `version.properties` rather than `build.gradle.kts` is deliberate: the watermark is `git log -L` on the version LINE, and the Gradle file is rewritten for reasons that release nothing, so the range would start at the wrong commit. **`0.3.0` is not arbitrary** — the last shipped APK (`kabmo-android-v0.2.0.apk`, whose `versionName` was the app tag) makes every installed shell report `KabmoAndroid/0.2.0`, so starting below it would make `isNewer` answer false for the first independent release and no installed app would ever be offered it. `versionCode` is still the workflow run number (`-PkabmoVersionCode`), because it is a monotonic counter rather than a version. `release/android.test.js` asserts the file, the build's use of it, the asset naming, the read/rewrite, and the watermark and range against a throwaway repository.
- **The bump must be COMMITTED by this job, not left for the app's release commit.** semantic-release's `@semantic-release/git` carries only its own `assets` (`CHANGELOG.md`, `package.json`, `bun.lock`), so a plugin bump left uncommitted is discarded when that job resets to its checkout — and the tarball would pack the OLD version. The commit is a `chore` (which `release/release-rules.js` gives `release: false`, so it cannot cut a version by itself) and carries `[skip ci]`.
- **`release/marketplace.js` holds the catalog; `release/watermark.js` holds the watermark itself (shared with the Android shell); `release/release-marketplace.mjs` is the runner.** The watermark is `git log -L` on the version LINE, and a tag cannot serve. These plugins are released by the APP's release, so a bump rides inside a `chore(release): vX.Y.Z` commit and there is no per-plugin tag to range from. `versionWatermark(dir, cwd)` asks git which commit last changed `"version"` in the manifest, and `commitsSinceWatermark` ranges from it — everything a plugin's history has to offer is either before the version it currently declares (already out) or after it (not yet). `-L` is what makes it correct: a `git log -p | grep` would report every commit that rewrote the file. An unresolvable watermark (shallow clone, no version line) reports every commit the caller collected rather than none, because "cannot tell" must never read as "nothing to do".
- **The bump LEVEL comes from the same rules as everything else.** `release/release-marketplace.mjs` runs the real `@semantic-release/commit-analyzer` with the config looked up by name from `release.config.mjs`, so a `fix` is a patch, a `feat` a minor and a `BREAKING CHANGE:` footer a major — verified on this tree: `session-info` computed **2.0.0** (its range contains `5018640`, whose body carries a real breaking footer) and `stats` **1.1.0**. A range of `chore`/`docs`/`test` commits asks for nothing and the version is left alone, so a plugin whose only change was a test file does not ship a no-op update to every user.
- **Only the bump is derived — no changelog, no tag, no GitHub release.** None of those reach an installed plugin, and `@semantic-release/changelog` would commit a file per plugin that nothing reads.
- **The write is surgical: two version LINES, never a `JSON.stringify` round-trip.** `replaceVersionLines` rewrites `"version"` (2-space indent) and `kabmo.version` (4-space) by anchoring on their indentation and the previous value, and throws unless it rewrote exactly two. A `JSON.stringify(manifest, null, 2)` round-trip is what `release/version-package.mjs` does for the packages and is wrong here: these manifests are hand-formatted, and re-serializing expanded every inline object — measured, a two-line bump came out as a 16-line diff rewriting `peerDependenciesMeta`.
- **Both version spellings must agree, and the gate says so.** `package.json` carries `version` and `kabmo.version`; the registry reads the SECOND (`toManifest` reads `pkg.kabmo`) and the sync compares it, so bumping only the first is a no-op that looks like a release. `release/marketplace.test.js` asserts they match in every manifest, that every directory under `marketplace/plugins/` has a `BUNDLED_PLUGINS` row (a plugin no row names is never bumped), that each row's `id` is the manifest's own id, and that every plugin stays `private` — plus the watermark and range against a throwaway repository, since a watermark is a fact about history and a test reading this repo's would break the moment a commit landed.

- **The gates and the browser specs are a REQUIREMENT of every release, and that is why they live in reusable workflows.** `.github/workflows/gates.yml` (the four gates, `bun test`, the generated settings schema) and `.github/workflows/e2e-specs.yml` (the Playwright suite) are `workflow_call` files; `ci.yml` and `e2e.yml` are now triggers that call them, and `release.yml`, `publish.yml` and `publish-plugins.yml` each have two jobs that call them and `needs:` them. A release job is therefore unreachable until both have passed. Re-running the commands inside `release.yml` would be a second copy that drifts from what a pull request runs, and reading the parallel CI run's conclusion over the API **races the run it is waiting for** — a release run starts at the same instant as the CI run, so "no run found yet" has to be told apart from "no run exists", and a red `main` read as a missing run is precisely the failure this shape prevents. `./.github/workflows/<file>.yml` is the reference form (same commit as the caller); `$/…` says the same thing but is newer, and `actionlint` rejects it — a parser that refuses the spelling fails the whole workflow, which on a release pipeline means no release at all, silently.
- **Each releasing workflow gates the commit it actually acts on, and the three are not the same commit.** `release.yml` gates the pushed commit (empty `ref:` input = the caller's `github.sha`); `publish-plugins.yml` gates `github.sha` too, but for a different reason — a tag push is a bare `git push` of a ref nobody gated, and these tags are pushed by `release.yml` onto the release commit, which is a LATER commit than the one `release.yml` itself gated; `publish.yml` passes `ref:` = the tag, because a `release: published` run is not a push to `main` (no `e2e.yml` run exists for that event to wait on at all) and a dispatch re-run must verify the published bytes rather than `main`'s tip. `release.yml` deliberately runs its gate jobs BEFORE each release job's `Sync to the current remote`: the sync can only fast-forward onto a release commit this same workflow produced, and that commit's own run gated its own starting point, so nothing ungated reaches a tag through it.
- **A reusable workflow RENAMES the checks a commit receives, and nothing can prevent it.** A called workflow's checks are namespaced by the CALLER's job name, so `<caller job> / <callee job>` is what reports: measured on this repository, the single job `Gates` in `ci.yml` now reports `Gates / Checks` and `Gates / Settings schema`, where it used to report `Gates` and `Settings schema`. That is inert HERE only because `main`'s ruleset requires no status checks (verified: its rules are `deletion` and `non_fast_forward`). A repository whose branch protection DID require those names would need the ruleset updated in the same change, or every pull request would wait forever on a check that no longer reports — and the callee's job name is not configurable from the caller, so there is no spelling that avoids it.
- **A gate job never carries an `if:`.** A gate with a condition is one that can be skipped, which is not a gate — the `e2e` label condition therefore stays on `e2e.yml`'s own caller job (the one context where a label exists) and a release always runs the browser layer. That matters because `e2e.yml` skips the browser suite on a pull request without the label, so the merge that produced a release commit may never have run one.
- **The iOS shell is the same product on the other mobile platform, and it shares the console's contract rather than restating it.** `packages/ios` is a SwiftPM package (Swift 6 + UIKit + WebKit, no SwiftUI, no third-party deps, no `.xcodeproj`) with the same two-target split `packages/macos` uses: `KabmoShell` holds every rule that can be tested without a screen (`URLNormalizer`, `ShellSettings` incl. the zoom mapping, `LoadingState`, `HealthProbe`, `ShellUpdate`, `ShellUserAgent`, `SVGPath`/`Wordmark`/`WordmarkRenderer`) and `kabmo-shell` is the UIKit app. The app target is guarded by `#if canImport(UIKit)` so `swift build` stays meaningful on a Mac with only the Command Line Tools — the logic suite (`swift run kabmo-shell-tests`, a plain executable because XCTest ships with Xcode) runs there and in CI, and the application only compiles against the iOS SDK. **Where the platform genuinely differs, the shell says so instead of imitating Android**: an IPA cannot be installed by an app (no `PackageInstaller` equivalent, no sideload-by-URL), so `ShellUpdate.action` recognises an IPA download and opens the release page with a reason instead of writing a file nothing can use; and `WKWebView.pageZoom` is already page-relative, so `ShellSettings.pageZoom` is a plain ratio — the Android twin's density multiply would render a 100% setting at 2.6× on a 3× phone, which `ShellSettingsTests` pins. **The launch overlay's rules are the Android ones**, including the two that fail silently: a progress frame after `done`/`failed` is ignored, and `didFinish` never clears a failure (a failed load still finishes on WebKit's error page). Progress arrives by KVO on `estimatedProgress` — `WKNavigationDelegate` reports started/finished/failed and nothing in between, so a delegate-only shell draws a bar that jumps from indeterminate to gone. **The app is named `Kabmo`, and the name is ENFORCED because nothing fails when it drifts.** `CFBundleDisplayName` and `CFBundleName` are both `Kabmo` (the Home Screen, the app switcher and Settings read the first), the bundle is `Kabmo.app`, and `CFBundleExecutable` is `Kabmo` — the FILE inside the bundle, which `scripts/build-ipa.sh` copies the SwiftPM product to, because the product stays `kabmo-shell` (SwiftPM derives it from the target, and a lower-case binary name is the platform's convention). Three files state it and each can be changed alone, so all three are checked: the build script refuses a bundle whose display name is not `Kabmo` and refuses an executable the bundle does not carry, `publish-ios.yml` re-checks both on the ARTIFACT (a stale checkout, or a later step rewriting the plist, passes the script's check and fails here), and `release/ios-name.test.js` pins the plist, the script, the workflow and the console's own `… for iOS` label. The quick action's title is deliberately NOT the app name: it reads `Server settings`, because a shortcut titled `Kabmo` would be the icon's label twice. The Android shell's `app_name`, the macOS/Linux/Windows shells' `Kabmo` window and executable names, and the PWA manifest's `name`/`short_name` are the same word — one product, one name. **A download keeps the session cookie, which is why it is a `WKDownload` and not a URL handed to Safari** (`ShellDownloads.swift`): the editor's file lives on the console's own origin and is authenticated by an `HttpOnly` cookie, so handing the URL to the system browser fetches the LOGIN PAGE — a corrupt-looking file rather than an authorization failure, the exact bug `KabmoDownloads` prevents on Android. `WKDownload` transfers with the web view's own cookie store and the finished file goes to the system share sheet; `ShellUpdate.safeFileName` makes a server-supplied name one path segment (a `Content-Disposition` with `../../etc/passwd` would otherwise resolve outside the download directory). An IPA is refused here too, by the same `ShellUpdate` decision. The wordmark is `public/kabmo-wordmark.svg` as path data (`Wordmark.swift`, generated, never hand-edited) and `WordmarkTests` re-derives every number from the SVG plus rasterizes it to assert ink where the artwork has ink — the same guard `packages/android`'s `WordmarkDrawableTest` applies to its VectorDrawable.
- **One console contract for both shells, and the prefix is the only per-shell fact.** `src/shared/lib/updates/shell-ua.ts` parses `KabmoAndroid/` and `KabmoIOS/` through ONE grammar (`shellVersion(ua, kind)`, `SHELL_UA_PREFIXES`), `src/server/lib/updates/shell-release.ts` resolves either artifact from the same release LIST (`fetchShellRelease(kind)`, per-shell cache on `globalThis`, `resetShellReleaseCache`), and `src/client/hooks/ui/updates/shell-download.ts` holds the one offer table both sidebars call (`useShellOffer`). The routes stay separate (`/api/updates/android`, `/api/updates/ios`) because one endpoint answering "the newest shell" would need a query parameter whose absence silently means Android. **There is no iOS INSTALL offer**: iOS has no sideload-by-URL path, so `shouldOfferShellDownload('ios', null, …)` returns null even on an iPhone — only an installed iOS shell is ever offered an update, and the Android browser offer is unchanged. The bootstrap carries two fields (`shellVersion`, `iosShellVersion`) rather than one "shell version", because the two artifacts are on separate release tracks and folding them would compare one shell's version against the other's published asset.
- **The Windows shell is released the same way, and for the same reason — `release/windows.js` + `release/release-windows.mjs`, run by the `windows` job.** `packages/windows/version.properties` is the ONE place its `versionName` is declared: `KabmoDesktop.App.csproj` reads it at build time through the `ReadShellVersion` target (into the assembly's `Version`, which is what the shell reports to the page as `shellVersion`), and `publish-windows.yml` names the asset from it. **The version is never written in the `.csproj`** — a literal there is a second source that drifts the first time either is bumped, and the failure is silent (the app reports a version the pipeline never published); `release/windows.test.js` fails on it. The target reads the file through `Regex` rather than `ReadLinesFromFile` + a `StartsWith` filter, because the item-metadata form evaluates to the LITERAL text `@(_VersionLine)` when the item is empty and the SDK rejects that with `NETSDK1018` (measured). **The artifact is an INSTALLER — `Kabmo-<v>-win-<arch>-setup.exe`, compiled from `packages/windows/installer/kabmo.iss` by Inno Setup 6**, which is preinstalled on `windows-latest` AND on `windows-11-arm` (verified against actions/runner-images: 6.7.1 on Windows 2022, 2025 and Windows 11 Arm64), so the build needs no third-party action and no download at run time. A ZIP would leave no Add/remove-programs entry, no Start-menu entry and no uninstaller, so "which version is this" and "how do I remove it" would both have no answer — the same reason `packages/macos` ships a `.app` and `packages/linux` a `.tar.gz` (each platform's own convention). Four properties are load-bearing and `release/windows.test.js` asserts each: a STABLE `AppId` (a generated one installs a second copy beside the first, with two Start-menu entries and two uninstallers), `PrivilegesRequired=lowest` (the app writes only to `%APPDATA%\Kabmo` and `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Kabmo\webview`, so the ordinary install shows no UAC prompt; `/ALLUSERS` overrides), a WebView2 check in `[Code]` before anything is copied (a missing runtime is why the window paints nothing, and saying so up front beats a blank window), and `MinVersion=10.0.17763` (the Evergreen Runtime's floor). The version arrives as `/DAppVersion=<v>` and the workflow re-reads it off the built file's own `ProductVersion` before uploading. It is NOT an MSIX: MSIX requires a certificate bound to a publisher identity and refuses to install without one, so the installer is unsigned and SmartScreen warns until it has reputation. **There is no User-Agent token, but the console DOES offer this app an update** — over the bridge, the same contract macOS uses (`__kabmoDesktop.shellVersion`, read from the assembly's own `Version`). Unlike the two mobile shells, nothing is downloaded FROM the console: those two are, so the server parses their published version on every request, while a desktop app is installed by hand and only needs to be TOLD it is behind.
- **The Linux shell is the fourth port of the same shell, and its two real differences from macOS are forced by the platform, not chosen.** `packages/linux` is a Rust crate (`gtk` 0.18 + `webkit2gtk` 2.0 with the `v2_22` feature floor, so Ubuntu 22.04 and Debian 12 are supported) split the way the Swift package is: `src/store.rs` holds the rules (server list, ownership by recorded pid, `normalise_url` refusing a base path), `src/system.rs` the side effects (probe, per-port record discovery, a `process_group(0)` spawn killed with `kill(-pgid)`), `src/main.rs` the window's app and wiring, with `src/chrome.rs` holding the window's own behaviour (shortcut table, `window.txt`, the webview's data directories, the page's drag/double-click/contextmenu intents) and `src/commands.rs` the commands the page may issue. The rules suite is a plain executable split by domain under `rules/` (`cargo run --bin kabmo-desktop-tests`, 78 checks, no display), because a rule that can only be checked by opening a window is a rule that is not checked. **The binary is named `Kabmo`, not the crate's name** — GTK derives the process name, `WM_CLASS` and the taskbar label from the executable's file name, so `kabmo-desktop` put that string in the window list while the window's own title said Kabmo (the same drift `packages/macos` fixed by naming its SwiftPM product `Kabmo`); `main` sets `prgname`/`application_name` from one `APP_NAME` constant before GTK starts, and `release/linux.test.js` asserts the title, the About panel, the launcher template's `Name=`/`StartupWMClass` and the binary's own file name together. **The installer is a real script, not a heredoc inside `build-app.sh`, and the `.desktop` is a TEMPLATE it fills in** (`scripts/install.sh` + `scripts/kabmo.desktop.in`): `Exec` takes an absolute path while a tarball unpacks anywhere, and the shipped-ready entry this replaced used `Exec=sh -c '…' sh %k`, which `desktop-file-validate` rejects outright (the spec has no single quotes, and `$` must be escaped inside a quoted argument) — an invalid entry fails SILENTLY, with the menu listing an app that does nothing when clicked. The `Exec` value is always QUOTED because a space is a reserved character there and the validator does NOT catch an unquoted one (measured: `Exec=/tmp/my prefix/bin/Kabmo` validates, and a launcher then splits it into two arguments), while `TryExec` stays a bare path for the inverse reason. The installer refuses to write an entry with an unresolved `@TOKEN@`, validates what it wrote, and has `--uninstall`; `publish-linux.yml` RUNS it into a throwaway prefix on every release and checks what it placed, so a token the script stopped filling in fails the build rather than the user's menu. Verified end to end: the workflow's own verify step run verbatim, a tarball unpacked into a random directory and installed from there, and `gtk-launch kabmo` starting the app from the generated entry. The release wiring is the other three shells' — `release/linux.js` + `release/release-linux.mjs` run by the `linux` job of `release.yml`, `publish-linux.yml` builds before the tag (`app` `needs: linux-build`) and only attaches after it (`linux-app`), and `packages/linux/version.properties` is the ONE place `versionName` is declared (`build.rs` bakes it in as `KABMO_SHELL_VERSION`; never `CARGO_PKG_VERSION`, because the crate is never published).
- **The three DESKTOP shells ship two architectures each, built NATIVELY, and every artifact is checked against its own header.** `macos` → `arm64` (`macos-15`) + `x64` (`macos-15-intel`); `windows` → `x64` (`windows-latest`) + `arm64` (`windows-11-arm`); `linux` → `x86_64` (`ubuntu-latest`) + `arm64` (`ubuntu-24.04-arm`) — every label verified in the GitHub-hosted runner reference, and each row compiles, runs its rules suite and verifies on the machine it targets. A cross-build is not an option: all three link native libraries (AppKit/WebKit, WPF/WebView2, GTK/WebKitGTK), so it would need a sysroot and would ship a binary nothing in the job ever executed. **The asset name carries the architecture, in one scheme for all three** — `<App>-<version>-<platform>-<arch>`: `Kabmo-<v>-mac-<arch>.zip` + `.dmg`, `Kabmo-<v>-win-<arch>-setup.exe`, and on Linux `Kabmo-<v>-linux-<arch>.tar.gz` / `.deb` / `.rpm` / `.AppImage` (+ `.zsync`). `Kabmo` is capitalised because it is the product's NAME (the word the window title, the installer and the `.desktop` entry carry), not a package slug; the two MOBILE shells keep `kabmo-android-v<v>.apk` / `kabmo-ios-v<v>.ipa` because `versionFromAssetName` PARSES those to answer "is a newer shell published", and renaming one would silently stop offering updates. Nothing parses a desktop name, which is why those three were free to change. **Each build asserts the architecture of the file it produced, from the file's own header** — `lipo -archs` (Mach-O), the PE `machine` field (`0x8664` x64 / `0xAA64` Arm64), the ELF `e_machine` field (62 x86-64 / 183 AArch64) — because a runtime pack or SDK that silently resolved to the host architecture would publish an `arm64`-named file carrying an x64 binary, which fails on the user's machine and nowhere in the pipeline. The macOS matrix carries a separate `macho` field (`x86_64`) because `lipo` prints a different spelling from the asset label (`x64`). The Windows installer's `ArchitecturesAllowed` comes from the same `/DAppArch` value that names the file — `x64os` for x64, `arm64` for Arm64 — so the Arm64 installer refuses an x64 machine and the x64 one refuses Arm64; `x64compatible` would have matched both via emulation, which is now the wrong file for an Arm machine to pick. Each build phase uploads one artifact PER architecture and the attach phase downloads them with `pattern:` + `merge-multiple: true`, then refuses to upload unless exactly two files arrived — a shared artifact name would let the second row overwrite the first, and a one-architecture release installs on one machine family and not the other.
  **Each desktop shell ships every package format its platform actually uses, and the Linux three are built by scripts beside `build-app.sh`.** macOS ships a `.zip` (what a script or updater consumes — `ditto`, so the signature and extended attributes survive) AND a `.dmg` (`hdiutil create` with the bundle beside an `Applications` alias, which is what a person expects to drag); `hdiutil` warns that it is deprecated in favour of `diskutil image create from`, and that alternative is unusable here because it takes no `-fs` — a folder source becomes an APFS volume, while HFS+ is what every macOS from 10.x up mounts. Linux ships `.tar.gz` (no root, `install.sh` → `~/.local`), `.deb` (`dpkg-deb`), `.rpm` (`rpmbuild`) and `.AppImage` (`appimagetool`, pinned, run with `APPIMAGE_EXTRACT_AND_RUN=1` because a container has no `/dev/fuse`) — four answers to "how do I install this", none of which replaces another. Two rules fail only on the user's machine and are therefore stated in the scripts: **the `.deb` depends on `libgtk-3-0t64`, not `libgtk-3-0`** (Ubuntu 24.04 renamed GTK 3's runtime package in the 64-bit `time_t` transition, and the old name makes the package UNINSTALLABLE — apt answers "no installation candidate"), and **the `.rpm` requires `gtk3` / `webkit2gtk4.1`**, the Fedora-family names, because the build host is Ubuntu and its own names resolve nowhere. `appimagetool` writes its `.zsync` into the CURRENT DIRECTORY, so the script runs it from inside the output directory — an absolute destination put the AppImage in the output dir and the delta metadata in the caller's cwd (measured). The `.deb` names its architecture `amd64`/`arm64` while RPM and AppImage say `x86_64`/`aarch64`, which is why the Linux matrix carries `deb` and `host` beside the asset label `arch`; the attach phase then requires TWO of EVERY format (`shopt -s nullglob`, because an unmatched pattern stays literal and would count 1) and treats the `.zsync` as optional. Release artifacts go to `artifacts/` — never `dist/` (the client bundle's own output, which a later build can delete) and never `release/` (tracked source: the bump scripts and their tests).
  **ALL FIVE shells now share one naming scheme** — `<App>-<version>-<platform>[-<arch>].<ext>`: `Kabmo-<v>-android.apk`, `Kabmo-<v>-ios.ipa`, `Kabmo-<v>-mac-<arch>.zip`/`.dmg`, `Kabmo-<v>-win-<arch>-setup.exe`, `Kabmo-<v>-linux-<arch>.tar.gz`/`.deb`/`.rpm`/`.AppImage`. The mobile pair has NO architecture segment because neither artifact is per-arch: the APK is one universal build (the shell is pure Kotlin, no native libraries — verified: no `.so`, no `jniLibs`, no `abiFilters`) and the IPA contains only the device slice (`arm64-apple-ios`). **The mobile rename needs a compatibility path and the desktop one does not**, because the console PARSES those two names to answer "is a newer shell published": `SHELL_ASSETS` carries `legacyPrefix`/`legacyExtension` (`kabmo-android-v`, `kabmo-ios-v`) and `versionFromAssetName` tries the current scheme first, then the legacy one. Without it an installed shell would read as "no update" from the moment this shipped — silently, since a name that does not match is indistinguishable from a release carrying no artifact. The two mobile prefixes are now both `Kabmo-`, so the EXTENSION is what keeps a desktop asset from being read as a mobile one; `shell-release.test.ts` pins every desktop filename as `null` for both kinds.
- **The IPA is released the same way the APK is — `release/ios.js` + `release/release-ios.mjs`, run by the `ios` job — and `publish-ios.yml` builds it on macOS.** `packages/ios/version.properties` is the ONE place the shell's `versionName` is declared: `scripts/build-ipa.sh` fills `CFBundleShortVersionString` from it through `release/ios-version.mjs`, the shell appends `KabmoIOS/<versionName>` to its WebView User-Agent through `applicationNameForUserAgent` (set on the CONFIGURATION — a value assigned to a live web view is ignored with no error, so the token would be missing from the very request that renders the shell), and the asset is named `Kabmo-<versionName>-ios.ipa` from the same file. The two-phase shape is the APK's: `ipa-build` runs BEFORE the tag and `app` `needs: ipa-build`, so a Swift error or an unavailable macOS runner stops the release before anything is tagged; `ipa` only attaches the run artifact. The bundle is assembled by hand (`scripts/build-ipa.sh`: no `xcodebuild`, icon rendered from geometry by `scripts/make-icon.swift`, icons verified against the plist's own list) and is **unsigned** — signing needs an Apple-issued certificate bound to a team and a device set, so the artifact is for a developer who re-signs it, for Xcode/TestFlight, and for CI verification. `release/ios.test.js` asserts the file, the build's use of it, the asset naming, the read/rewrite, the watermark and range against a throwaway repository, AND that the Swift prefix, the TypeScript prefix and the asset naming agree — three files stating two strings, where a change to one alone makes the console read the shell as a browser.

- **The Android APK is a RELEASE artifact, not an npm package — and BUILDING it is a GATE on the app release.** `packages/android` is a private Kotlin application, so there is no registry for it: `.github/workflows/publish-android.yml` is a reusable body (the same shape as `publish-app.yml`) whose only output is the `.apk` attached to the GitHub release. **It runs in two phases, and the split is the point.** `build` compiles, verifies and uploads the APK as a run artifact; `release.yml`'s `apk-build` job calls it with `mode: build`, `ref: main` and `version-code: ${{ github.run_number }}`, and `app` `needs: apk-build` — so a toolchain that moved, a missing keystore, a Kotlin error or a signature mismatch stops the release BEFORE a tag exists. `attach` downloads that artifact and uploads it to the release `app` created; `release.yml`'s `apk` job calls it with `mode: attach`, `needs: [app, apk-build]` and the same `if: needs.app.outputs.released == 'true'` as `app-publish`. Artifacts are scoped to the workflow RUN, so the two calls are one pipeline: the attach uploads the exact bytes the checks passed and cannot rebuild them. **The old shape built the APK after the tag**, so a failure there left a release already tagged and published with no shell attached — measured: the SDK's `tools` package was removed and the job died in setup, so the console kept offering the previous APK and the only repair was a hand-run dispatch. Building before the tag inverts that. `ref: main` rather than the trigger commit is load-bearing: the `android` bump job just pushed the shell version, and building the starting commit would declare the PREVIOUS one — an update no installed app is ever offered. A manual `workflow_dispatch` builds the TAG it names (so a repair matches the release it repairs) and attaches when given one; there is no `mode` input on that path, because a dispatch has no earlier call in its own run to have uploaded an artifact. It does NOT call the gate workflows: it is reachable only from `release.yml`, whose `app` job already depends transitively on both, and re-running the browser matrix for a packaging step adds minutes without coverage. That is why `tests/ci-gates.test.ts` treats it as a body that is NOT a gate provider and asserts the `needs:` chain instead. **`permissions: contents: write` must be on the CALLER of the attach** — a reusable workflow can only downgrade what it inherits, so `read` in `release.yml` turns the body's `write` into read and the upload fails with 403 after a full build; the build call needs only `contents: read`. **`version-code` is `${{ github.run_number }}`, typed `string`, stated at BUILD time and nowhere else**: Android refuses an install whose `versionCode` does not increase, so a fixed value makes every release after the first uninstallable over the previous one — and `github.run_number` is a STRING context value, so a `number` input rejects it (actionlint: "string value cannot be assigned"). The `versionName` comes from `packages/android/version.properties` — the shell's OWN version, bumped by `release/release-android.mjs` in the `android` job of `release.yml` — and only `versionCode` reaches Gradle as a property (`-PkabmoVersionCode`), because it is a monotonic counter rather than a version. The built APK is verified with `aapt2 dump badging` against both, and the asset is named `kabmo-android-v<versionName>.apk` from the same file, because `versionFromAssetName` reads the published version out of the asset NAME. Signing material is REQUIRED: without `ANDROID_KEYSTORE_BASE64` the build step FAILS the job rather than falling back to a debug key (a different key on every runner, so no APK it produced could install over the previous one), and the built certificate is checked against the keystore's own fingerprint; the emptiness check lives in the SHELL because the `secrets` context is not available in a step `if:`. The keystore and `keystore.properties` are removed in an `always()` step.
- **The shell button is TWO offers from TWO facts, and conflating them hides one of them.** It sits in the sidebar footer directly after *About* (both layouts). **`update`**: the page is running inside the APK and a newer one is published. **`install`**: the page is an Android BROWSER (Chrome, Brave, Firefox) and an APK is published — the reader may not know the native shell exists, which is the whole point of the offer, and there is no installed version to compare against so it names the newest published one. A desktop browser and an iPad get neither: an APK cannot be installed there, and the app's own update badge already covers the server. `ShellDownload.kind` carries which one it is, and `shellDownloadLabel` is the single place the wording lives (`Install …` vs `Update …`) because both footers draw the control — the phone's visible word is `Get APK` / `Update APK` rather than the old `APK`, which named the file type instead of the action. Both facts travel on the **User-Agent**, and they are different questions: `KabmoWebView.configure` appends `KabmoAndroid/<versionName>` and `androidShellVersion` parses it into the bootstrap's `shellVersion` (the app), while `isAndroidDevice` matches the platform token into `isAndroid` (the device). The UA is the only channel that crosses the very request which renders the shell, so the page is served already knowing and no bridge or extra round trip is needed. **`isAndroidDevice` matches `Android`, never `Mobile`**: an Android tablet sends `Android` without `Mobile` and is a device the APK installs on, while an iPad sends `Mobile` and has no APK at all — a `Mobile` test would drop the first and admit the second. Measured in the browser across six user agents: Chrome-Android and Brave-Android → `Install Kabmo 0.3.5 for Android` (`Get APK`), shell `0.3.0` against a published `0.3.5` → `Update Kabmo 0.3.5 for Android` (`Update APK`), shell `0.3.5` → nothing, desktop Chrome → nothing, iPad → nothing. The second comes from `GET /api/updates/android` → `fetchAndroidRelease` (`src/server/lib/updates/android-release.ts`), one unauthenticated GitHub read of the release LIST — **not `/releases/latest`**, which answers the newest release of ANY kind and this repository publishes THREE artifacts from one push. The packages are released FIRST (`packages → marketplace → android → apk-build → app`), and `@semantic-release/github` runs in both pipelines, so a `kabmo-sdk/v*` release is newer than the app release that carries the APK; measured against a stub answering exactly that shape, `/releases/latest` produced `{url: null, latest: null}` and the button vanished while the APK sat one entry below. The probe therefore scans the list (20 entries, drafts and prereleases skipped) and takes the first one whose ASSET LIST names an APK,  cached 5 minutes on `globalThis` (the `bun --hot` rule) with `resetShellReleaseCache()` as the test seam. **The version is read from the asset NAME, never the release tag** — the APK rides on the app's release, whose tag names the SERVER version, so a tag comparison would compare an APK against a server. An up-to-date app shows nothing, and so does one AHEAD of the published asset (a sideloaded newer build): the `update` branch requires `isNewer(latest, shellVersion)`, while the `install` branch needs no comparison at all because nothing is installed. **The shell token alone is enough for the update offer even when `isAndroid` is absent** — both come from the same UA, so a bootstrap served by an older build must not silently drop the update. A failed probe answers `{url: null, latest: null}` and never a 500, because a button the console cannot back with a link must not appear. Measured end to end in the real APK against a stub: `shellVersion: 0.1.0` + release `9.9.9` → button after About with the right href; release `0.1.0` → no button, footer reads Settings · About · Desktop; a plain browser → no button at all.
- **The DESKTOP trio installs its own update too, and the whole path exists because the shell now reports its ARCHITECTURE.** Every desktop artifact is per-architecture (`Kabmo-0.2.0-mac-arm64.zip`, `Kabmo-0.2.0-win-x64-setup.exe`, `Kabmo-0.2.0-linux-x86_64.AppImage`), so the console used to answer a desktop probe with the RELEASE PAGE — correct for a link, useless for a self-update, because naming one arch's file hands half the readers a build that will not launch. The shell injects `arch` beside `shellVersion` in `__kabmoDesktop` (a COMPILE-TIME fact in all three: `#if arch(arm64)`, `RuntimeInformation.ProcessArchitecture`, `cfg!(target_arch)`, which is also the right answer for a Rosetta/emulated launch), `useShellDownload` sends it as `?arch=` to `/api/updates/desktop?kind=…`, and `fetchShellRelease(kind, fetch, arch)` resolves that ONE file. **`direct` is a FIELD on the release payload**, not something the client infers: it is true for the mobile pair (one file for every device) and for a desktop shell that named its arch, and false for a desktop probe without one — which is exactly the link-versus-button decision. `SHELL_ASSETS[kind].selfUpdate` is the one table stating WHICH file a shell can install by itself: the `.zip` for macOS (`ditto -x -k` extracts it with its signature; a `.dmg` cannot be extracted without mounting), the Inno `-setup.exe` for Windows (a stable `AppId` is what makes an install an UPGRADE), the `.AppImage` for Linux (the only one of its four formats a shell can replace — `.deb`/`.rpm` need root). An arch the release does not publish keeps the PAGE rather than falling back to the sibling build, and the arch is part of the CACHE KEY (`shell-cache.ts`, `kind:arch`), because one console is routinely shared by machines that differ only by architecture. **`ShellUpdateButton` (`common/shell-update-button/`) is one control on both layouts, and the footer carries NO status text — every word a run has to say is a dialog.** `canSelfUpdate` is the one predicate: a FILE, in a DESKTOP shell, inside the shell — Android installs through `PackageInstaller` (its own path, untouched) and iOS cannot install at all, so neither belongs here, and a shell that cannot install it draws a LINK to the release page instead. **The LINK draws the glyph too, in both variants — that branch is the mobile pair's ONLY branch.** `canSelfUpdate` refuses Android and iOS (each installs through its own path, or cannot install at all), so every APK/IPA offer is a link, and the link used to render `variant === 'label'` as the word alone: the phone's footer showed `Get APK` with no mark beside it while Settings, About and Desktop all had one. The `label` variant's glyph is **14px** and its class is `LABEL_CLASS` — the footer's own bare row, no `p-1` and no hover plate — because the desktop's 16px padded glyph plate reads as a different kind of control in a row of bare glyph-and-word entries; the icon-only variant keeps `ICON_CLASS` at 16. `index.test.tsx` pins the link case (an `<svg>` inside the `<a>`, the word beside it, no `p-1`) and `mobile.e2e.ts` measures the rendered box against the About glyph's own width, because a DOM query would pass on an `<svg>` with no box. The button opens `Modal.tsx` in a **`createPortal` to `document.body`**, and the portal is load-bearing twice: the footer is a 24px flex row of glyphs, so a dialog nested in it put its whole sentence into the ROW's own text content (measured in the browser — the row read as the dialog body), and any `overflow`/`transform` later added to that row would clip a `fixed` overlay. Four phases live in the dialog and nowhere else — **`confirm`** (what the platform will do: macOS replaces the bundle, Windows runs the installer silently, Linux swaps the AppImage and says a `.deb`/`.rpm` install cannot; plus what is NOT lost, since only this window restarts), **`working`**, and the shell's own sentence for **`done`** / **`error`** with a `Try again` that re-runs the same offer. **A run is never started by the footer control** — the confirmation is what asks the shell, which is why a 24px glyph cannot replace the app on a single press with no words. `reset()` runs when the dialog OPENS, so a dismissed failure does not greet the next press with its old refusal; `reset` refuses while a run is in flight, because the shell is already replacing itself and reporting `idle` would re-arm a control whose second press starts a second installer. Closing mid-run hides the dialog without cancelling anything — the shell has already begun, and a Cancel that could not stop it would be a button that lies — so the control stays disabled until the run answers. **The shell does the work, and the reply is sent BEFORE it quits** — a page whose shell vanished mid-request would keep a spinner over an update that worked: macOS downloads, `ditto`-extracts, checks `codesign --verify --deep --strict` plus the bundle's identifier and a STRICTLY higher version, renames the old bundle aside (undoing it on failure so the user is never left with no app) and restarts via a detached waiter that waits for its own pid; Windows downloads, checks `MZ`, runs the installer `/SILENT /SUPPRESSMSGBOXES /NORESTART /CLOSEAPPLICATIONS /SP-` and closes 600 ms later so the reply lands; Linux requires `$APPIMAGE` (a `.deb`/`.rpm` install says so and offers the page), checks the ELF `e_machine`, stages BESIDE the image so the `rename` stays on one filesystem, and schedules the relaunch one second later. **What none of them can promise, and each module says so: authenticity.** The macOS build is signed AD-HOC and the Windows/Linux artifacts are unsigned, so these checks catch a truncated download, an HTML error page saved as a binary and the other architecture's build — they do NOT prove provenance. HTTPS from an allowlisted GitHub host is what bounds the damage meanwhile; Developer ID + notarisation and Authenticode are the pipeline changes that would make it a real check. Rules live in testable modules (`packages/macos/Sources/KabmoDesktop/ShellUpdate.swift`, `packages/windows/src/KabmoDesktop/ShellUpdate.cs`, `packages/linux/src/update.rs`) and are pinned by `swift run kabmo-desktop-tests` (157 checks), `dotnet run --project packages/windows/tests` (124) and the Linux rules suite — all three suites assert the same host allowlist, the same version comparison and the same refusal sentences, so the four implementations cannot drift about whether an update exists.

- **Tapping that button inside the APK finishes the install itself — one system dialog, no file manager — and the keystore is what makes it possible at all.** `ApkInstaller` downloads the APK into the app's cache, verifies it (`getPackageArchiveInfo`: the package must be `dev.kabmo.shell` and its `versionCode` must be strictly HIGHER than the installed one, because Android refuses an equal code with `INSTALL_FAILED_VERSION_DOWNGRADE`), then commits one `PackageInstaller` session; `InstallResultReceiver` launches the system's own "Do you want to update this app?" from `STATUS_PENDING_USER_ACTION` and reports every terminal status back through the same vocabulary. **Silent install is impossible for a sideloaded app** — only a device-owner or Play-managed one replaces itself without consent — so the confirmation stays, and `REQUEST_INSTALL_PACKAGES` plus the per-app "Allow from this source" toggle is what removes the four-step Downloads dance. The rules that fail silently live in `ApkUpdate` and are JVM-tested: what counts as an APK (URL path, MIME, or a `Content-Disposition` that NAMES a file — never the fallback name, or every download would match), which installer status is which failure, and that a download name is sanitized to one path segment. `MainActivity.onDestroy` releases the flow's static dialog and the installer callback, or an install that outlives the Activity crashes a successful update with `BadTokenException`.
- **The update path only works because every release is signed by the SAME keystore, and CI now refuses to ship one that is not.** `v0.2.0`, `v0.3.0` and `v0.4.0` each carried a different certificate — `apksigner verify --print-certs` on the published assets shows three distinct SHA-256 digests — because `ANDROID_KEYSTORE_BASE64` was unset and `build.gradle.kts` falls back to `~/.android/debug.keystore`, which a runner regenerates per job. Measured on an emulator with the real assets: `adb install kabmo-android-v0.4.0.apk` over 0.3.0 answers `INSTALL_FAILED_UPDATE_INCOMPATIBLE: Existing package dev.kabmo.shell signatures do not match newer version`, and the same pair built with one keystore installs cleanly (`versionCode` 17 → 18, `Success`), while a downgrade is refused (`INSTALL_FAILED_VERSION_DOWNGRADE`). `publish-android.yml` therefore FAILS when the secret is absent (the fallback remains for a contributor's local build, never for a release) and verifies the built APK's certificate against the keystore's own fingerprint — `keytool` prints it uppercase and `apksigner` lowercase, so both are folded before comparing. `version.properties` carries `0.5.0` for the migration: one uninstall is still needed to get onto a device holding an older build, and every release after that updates in place.
- **The WebView's pinch zoom and the Settings → Page zoom slider are DIFFERENT zooms, and the first build had pinch switched off.** `setSupportZoom(true)` + `builtInZoomControls(true)` + `displayZoomControls(false)` is the browser-shaped combination: the gesture works, no +/- buttons appear. The shell shipped with the first two `false`, so a two-finger spread did nothing while Chrome zoomed — the reported "kenapa gak ngezoom kayak Chrome".
